ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS PART I-SCOPE Section 2. Statutory definitions. 

§ 2(2)-Combined Business and Dwelling Uses Where Business and Dwelling Portions Are Rented to Single Tenant but Are Separable. Administrator's Opinion No. 33 [Collated in Administrator's Opinion 

No. 115 under this section]. 

§ 2(2) Combined Business and Dwelling Uses Where Business and Dwelling Portions Are Rented to a Single Tenant but Are Not Separable. 

Administrator's Opinion No. 34 [Collated in Administrator's Opinion 

No. 115 under this section]. 

§ 2(2)-Combined Business and Dwelling Uses Where Space Within a Structure Is Rented to a Separate Tenant for Dwelling Purposes. 

Administrator's Opinion No. 35. 

A property containing a store and kitchen on the first floor and dwelling rooms on the second floor was previously rented to a single tenant. The dwelling portion, comprising the rooms on the second floor and the kitchen on the first floor, was not separable from the business portion, and the store comprised a predominant part of the total space, so that the property as a whole was not subject to the Regulations. The landlord now rents the store to one tenant and the dwelling space to a separate tenant. What portion, if any, is subject to the Regulations? 1 Under these circumstances, the dwelling portion is subject to the Regulations. Similarly, if the property as a whole is rented to a single tenant, and that tenant sublets the dwelling portion, the dwelling portion becomes subject to the Regulations. The maximum rent will be de termined pursuant to Section 36 of the Regulations. 

If any space within a structure is rented to a separate tenant for dwelling purposes, whether by sub-lease or otherwise, such space is subject to the Regulations independently of the status of the structure as a whole or of any other part of the structure. 249

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

§ 2(2)-Combined Business and Dwelling Uses Where There Is a Change from Dwelling to Business Use. Administrator's Opinion No. 36. 

Certain premises were rented for dwelling purposes and were so occupied on March 1, 1950. Thereafter, the tenant began to operate a business on the premises. Do they continue subject to the Regulations?

The status of the property must be determined on the basis of the new use. If the space used by the tenant for business purposes is separable from the portion used for dwelling purposes, then only the latter portion remains subject to the Regulations. If the space used for business purposes is not separable from the dwelling portion and is a predominant part of the total space, then the entire property ceases to be subject to the Regulations. 

$2(2)-Nursing and Convalescent Home. See Administrator's Opinion No. 44 Under Section 9(10). 

§ 2(2)-Combined Business and Dwelling Uses Where Premises Are Rented to a Single Tenant. Administrator's Opinion No. 99 [Collated in Administrator's Opinion No. 115 under this section]. 

§ 2(2) 

Combined Business and Dwelling Uses Where Premises Are Rented to a Single Tenant. Administrator's Opinion No. 115. 

A landlord requested advice as to whether premises which are rented by a commercial lease to a tenant who uses them for combined business and dwelling purposes are subject to the Rent and Eviction Regulations.

In view of the fact that there have been several policy changes, the determination whether housing accommodations which are used partially for dwelling purposes and partially for commercial or professional purposes are subject to control under the Emergency Housing Rent Control

Law and the Rent and Eviction Regulations is one which depends upon the date when the housing accommodations were first put to this combined use. 

This opinion is not applicable to those cases where, as a 250

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

device to evade the Regulations, a landlord rents an apartment under a commercial lease although the apartment is to be used solely for dwelling purposes. The device of using a commercial lease agreement in order to secure more than the maximum rent prescribed for the apartment will not deprive the tenant of his rights under the Act or the Regulations. This opinion is therefore addressed solely to those cases where there actually is a combined business and dwelling use of the housing accommodations.

On May 1, 1950, when the Commission first assumed control over residential rentals in New York State, the Administrator followed the policy previously adopted by the Federal authorities with respect to the combined business and dwelling use of housing accommodations. This policy was stated in Administrator's Opinions Nos. 33 and 34 and the primary test under those opinions was whether the business and dwelling portions were separable. If they were separable, then Administrator's Opinion No. 33 was applicable. If they were not separable, then Adminis trator's Opinion No. 34 governed. 

On August 25, 1953 the Administrator modified his policy with respect to the combined business and dwelling use of an apartment where the portions were not separable. 

In making this change he adopted the position of the Appellate Division of the Supreme Court in the case of Vojda v. Prizep (113 NYS2d 681), where the Court based its finding that the apartment was not subject to the Rent and Eviction Regulations upon the fact that the tenant derived a substantial income from the use of the apartment in her business. The Administrator then adopted the policy of exempting housing accommodations from the Regulations where the landlord was acting in good faith and was not using a commercial lease as a device to evade the Regulations, and the tenant actually derived a substantial income 251

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

from the use of the apartment in the practice of his profession, business or vocation. This policy was incorporated in Administrator's Opinion No. 99 which applies to the renting of housing accommodations for combined business and dwelling use on and after August 25, 1953 where the business and dwelling portions are not separable. 

Later Section 13 was added to the Regulations, effective May 1, 1955, in order to afford the Administrator a more effective means to control evasions of the Regulations by the device of using a commercial lease. On and after May 

1, 1955 even housing accommodations which are legitimately rented for a combined business and dwelling use continue to remain under control until the Administrator issues an order exempting them for the period of the tenant's occupancy. Section 13 is applicable to both situations. 

The separability of the business and dwelling portions is immaterial where the premises are rented for a combined use on or after May 1, 1955.

The varying opinions of the Administrator dealing with this subject matter have been collated into a single Administrator's Opinion in order to provide the public with a single opinion covering the various situations. 

The foregoing outlines the historical development of the policy adopted by the Administrator. This policy has not been given retroactive effect and renting of housing accommodations for such combined use during the periods when other policies were extant are governed by the policy then in effect. These policies are as follows: 252

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS COMBINED BUSINESS AND DWELLING USES WHERE BUSINESS AND DWELLING PORTIONS ARE RENTED TO A SINGLE TENANT BUT ARE SEPARABLE (formerly ADMINISTRATOR'S OPINION NO. 33) 

(applies to such renting at any time prior to May 1, 1955) Where the business and dwelling portions are rented to a single tenant but are separable from each other, only the dwelling portion is subject to the Regulations. An apportionment of rent to the dwelling portion must be made in order to establish the maximum rent. Such apportionment must be fair and reasonable. The landlord may, in the first instance, make such apportionment which will be subject to review by the Local Rent Administrator under Section 36 of the Regulations. Where the landlord has not made any apportionment, the Local Rent Administrator may do so on application of the tenant or on his own initiative under Section 36.

The business and dwelling portions are clearly separable where they are in separate structures. Where both portions are in the same structure they are separable if the following conditions exist: (1) There are separate means of access; and (2) The business and dwelling portions are each usable for their respective purposes without access to the other portion; and (3) The dwelling portion is, or can readily be, completely shut off from the business portion. 

Where premises do not in some particular respect comply with the test of separability just stated, they may nevertheless be found separable if it is very clear that in accordance with the standards and practices of the community, 253

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

it is feasible for the tenant to occupy the dwelling portion while the business portion is used by some other person. 

COMBINED BUSINESS AND DWELLING USES WHERE BUSINESS AND DWELLING PORTIONS ARE RENTED TO A SINGLE TENANT BUT ARE NOT SEPARABLE (formerly ADMINISTRATOR'S OPINION NO. 34) 

(applies to such renting at any time prior to August 25, 1953) Where the business and dwelling portions are rented to a single tenant, but are not separable, then both portions have a single combined status determined in accordance with the following rules: (1) If a predominant part of the total space is used for business purposes, the property is not subject to the Regu lations. 

(2) Where less than a predominant part of the total space is used for business purposes (and also where the space test cannot be used because there is no physical segregation of the space used for business purposes and that used for dwelling purposes) but the rental value of the business portion (or of the business use where the two uses are not physically segregated) is clearly in excess of the rental value of the dwelling portion, the property is not subject to the Regulations. (3) If less than a predominant part of the total space is used for business purposes, and the rental value of the business portion (or business use) does not clearly exceed the rental value of the dwelling portion (or dwelling use), the entire property is subject to the Regulations. 254

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS COMBINED BUSINESS AND DWELLING USE WHERE PREMISES ARE RENTED BY COMMERCIAL LEASE TO TENANT (formerly ADMINISTRATOR'S OPINION NO. 99) 

(applies to such renting during the period from August 25, 1953 to May 1, 1955)

Where the landlord is acting in good faith and is not using the commercial lease as a device to evade the Rent Regulations, and the tenant does actually derive a substantial income from the use of the apartment in the practice of his profession, business, or vocation, the housing accommodations are not subject to the Rent and Eviction Regulations.

There is a wide variety of bona fide situations in which apartments are rented for combined commercial and dwelling purposes. The following are a few typical cases and illustrate the Administrator's position relative thereto : 

1. A music teacher rents a studio apartment by a commercial lease which specifically states that the tenant will use the accommodations as his music studio for the purpose of giving musical instructions. The tenant is also given the right to use the apartment for dwelling purposes. The tenant submits his references to the landlord and establishes that he is a music teacher, and that the accommodations will be used as a music studio. The tenant does have pupils come to his apartment and does derive substantial income from the use of the apartment as his music studio. The tenant has no other music studio and the address of the apartment appears on his professional stationery and whatever advertising media is used by him. The tenant now requests a ruling of the Local Rent Administrator that the accommodations are subject to the Regulations since that portion of the studio apartment which is used exclusively 255

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

for dwelling purposes is greater in area than the area used for musical instructions. Are the housing accommodations subject to the Rent and Eviction Regulations?

No. Since the landlord in good faith has entered into a commercial lease with the tenant, and since the tenant does derive substantial income from the practice of his profession within the accommodations, the leased premises are not subject to the Regulations. Also relevant to this policy determination is the fact that the students of the tenant visit the apartment for the purpose of taking lessons and thereby use the common facilities of the building for such purposes.

However, if the tenant, while a musician, was not a music teacher and did not derive at the time of the renting, or thereafter, any income from the teaching of music in the apartment, but used the housing accommodations exclusively for dwelling purposes, they would be subject to the Regulations. The fact that the tenant might practice his musical instrument within the housing accommodations would not affect the basic nature of the housing accommodations. Similarly, an apartment used by a professional writer who does his writing in his home, or a commercial artist who does some of his office work at his home, would be deemed housing accommodations and subject to the Regulations, since in neither case is there the element of usage by people who come to consult the tenant in his professional capacity and pay fees therefor. 

2. An apartment is rented in good faith by a landlord to an architect for the purpose of combined professional and housing use. The architect has no other office and does, from the time of the inception of the tenancy, use the apartment as his office and does derive a substantial income from the practice of his profession. Subsequently the architect rents an office elsewhere, and now requests the Local Rent 256

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

Administrator to rule that the accommodations are subject to the Regulations, since they are now used solely as housing accommodations. Are the accommodations subject to the Regulations?

No. Where a commercial lease is executed and the tenant from the inception of the lease does use the apartment for a combined housing and professional, or commercial purposes, a subsequent abandonment by the tenant of the professional or commercial use will not bring the apartment under the control of the Regulations. It would be inequitable to permit a tenant to thus unilaterally change the rental agreement in order to secure the protection of the Regulations. 

3. An apartment is rented under a commercial lease to a tenant who teaches in a local college and who uses only one room of the apartment for the preparation of his teaching assignments. The lease contains a provision that "The demised premises shall be used by the tenant for his business as a professional teacher and that incidental to such professional use the tenant shall have the right to use a portion of the premises as a strictly private dwelling for himself and his immediate family only." The tenant derives no income from the use of the apartment. Is the apartment subject to the Rent and Eviction Regulations?

Yes. An apartment rented under a commercial lease to a tenant who does not from the inception of his lease derive any income from the use of the apartment to practice his profession or to conduct any business activities therein and uses the apartment primarily for residential purposes, is subject to the Rent and Eviction Regulations. An attempt by the landlord to evict the tenant pursuant to Section 52 of the Regulations on the basis that there has been a breach of a substantial obligation of the tenancy would be deemed 

—17 

257

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

to be a violation of Section 51(2) of the Regulations which provides as follows: 

"2. It shall be unlawful for any person to remove or attempt to remove any tenant or occupant from any housing accommodations or to refuse to renew the lease or agreement for the use of such accommodations, because such tenant or occupant has taken, or proposes to take, action authorized or required by the Act or any regulation, order or requirement thereunder."

COMMERCIAL OR PROFESSIONAL RENTING OF APARTMENT ON OR AFTER MAY 1, 1955 

Section 13 of the Regulations provides that "Any housing accommodation subject to these Regulations which may be rented on or after May 1, 1955 for commercial or professional use shall continue to be subject to control and the landlord may not collect more than the maximum rent until an order is issued by the Administrator exempting the housing accommodation from these Regulations during the period of occupancy by the tenant. Such order shall be issued by the Administrator where he finds that the renting complies with the requirements of state and local authorities and was made in good faith and without any intent to evade the Act or these Regulations and shall be effective as of the date of the commercial or professional renting." In applying this section to the facts of an individual case the Administrator will be governed by the same standards as were previously used in determining whether the landlord is acting in good faith and the tenant is deriving a substantial income from the use of the apartment in the practice of his profession, business or vocation. 258

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS § 2(7)—Definition of Tenant in Eviction Proceedings. Administrator's Opinion No. 61. 

A one-family dwelling occupied by the seller was sold on June 1, 1950. The contract of sale provided that the seller may continue in possession of the premises for a period of 60 days after the delivery of the deed. It was agreed that the seller, during the 60 days, would pay the purchaser for the use and occupation of the premises and further, that the relationship of landlord and tenant would not thereby be created. More than 60 days have elapsed since the delivery of the deed and the seller has refused to deliver possession to the purchaser. Is the seller a tenant entitled to the protection of the Rent and Eviction Regulations?

No. In a proceeding brought by the purchaser against the seller to enforce compliance with the terms of a sales agreement, the purchaser would not be required to obtain a Certificate of Eviction before commencing court proceedings to obtain possession of the one-family dwelling. The seller in possession in such a case is not considered a tenant within the meaning of the Rent and Eviction Regu lations. 

§ 2(7)-Payment of Rent by Janitor. See Administrator's Opinion No. 

42 Under Section 10(2). 

Section 3. Additional definitions. 

§ 3(8)—Change of Room by Hotel Tenant Before March 1, 1950. Admin istrator's Opinion No. 52 (For New York City and Buffalo, only).

A tenant in a hotel has been residing there since December 2, 1949. However, between December 2, 1949 and

March 1, 1950, the tenant had moved from one room to another room in the same hotel. He occupied that room on March 1, 1950, and has been in continuous occupancy 259

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

of that room since that date. Is the tenant protected by the 

Rent and Eviction Regulations?

Yes. Since the tenant has resided continuously in the hotel for 90 days or more prior to March 1, 1950, he is protected by the Regulations. His maximum rent is the rent paid for the particular room he occupied on March 1, 1950. 

§ 3(8) Change of Room by Hotel Tenant After March 1, 1950. Admin istrator's Opinion No. 53 (For New York City and Buffalo, only).

A tenant lived in a hotel room since December 2, 1949. 

After March 1, 1950, the tenant moved into a different unit in the same hotel. Is the tenant protected by the

Rent and Eviction Regulations in his occupancy of the new room?

Where the tenant requested that he be permitted to move to another room to suit his own convenience, and the tenant was fully advised by the hotel that he would cease to be a protected hotel tenant upon his removal to such other room, the tenant loses the protection of the Rent and Eviction Regulations if he moves. If there is a dispute between the tenant and the hotel as to the facts and circumstances surrounding the change in rooms, the Local Rent Administrator may institute a proceeding pursuant to Section 36(a) of the Regulations to determine the facts. 

However, where the tenant changed his room to another room at the request or for the convenience of the hotel, he continues to be protected by the Regulations in his occupancy of the new room. The maximum rent for his new room is the amount he agrees to pay for it provided that the amount is not in excess of: (a) The rent previously registered with the Office of the Housing Expediter for that particular room for the same number of persons and term of occupancy; or 2602

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

(b) The amount of the maximum rent for the new room as previously determined under the Rent and Eviction Regulations provided no change is being made in the number of persons or term of occupancy; or (c) The amount of the maximum rent determined under Section 23 of the Rent and Eviction Regulations including any equalization adjustment authorized in that section. 

If none of these three formulas is applicable, the maximum rent shall be established by the Local Rent Administrator pursuant to Section 36(d) upon the basis of the maximum rents for comparable controlled housing accommodations within the hotel after taking into consideration all other factors bearing on the equities involved. 

§ 3(8)—Change in Number of Occupants Caused by Hotel Tenant After March 1, 1950. Administrator's Opinion No. 53A (For New York 

City and Buffalo, only).

An unmarried woman has lived in a hotel room since 

December 2, 1949. She has now married and her husband shares the room with her. Does the tenant continue to have the protection of the Rent and Eviction Regulations as long as she lives in the room with her husband? 

The wife is a protected hotel tenant since she has been continuously in occupancy from December 2, 1949 to the present date. Although the husband is not a protected hotel tenant having moved into the room after December 2, 1949, the room or rooms occupied by the wife are subject to control since they are housing accommodations which have been and still are occupied by a tenant who has resided in the hotel continuously since December 2, 1949.

The hotel may file an application pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Regulations for an increase in rent based upon the increased services now being rendered because of the double occupancy. Where the hotel can establish a prac- 261

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

tice of customarily varying rents for one or two person occupancy, the hotel may obtain an adjustment reflecting such regular and customary increase. However, where the hotel cannot establish such a practice of customarily varying rents, the Local Rent Administrator may grant an adjustment not to exceed 25% of the maximum rent which the tenant paid while she alone occupied the room, after taking into consideration the equities involved. 

Section 9. Housing accommodations not subject to rent control. 

§ 9-Limited Dividend Housing. Administrator's Opinion No. 59. 

A tenant filed a complaint with a Local Rent Office because his landlord refused to paint his apartment. The apartment is located in a building owned by a private owner and was constructed pursuant to the Public Housing Law. 

The rentals for the apartments were fixed by the State Commissioner of Housing in accordance with such law. 

What action can the Local Rent Administrator take upon the tenant's complaint?

Section 2(2) (e) of the Emergency Housing Rent Control 

Law provides that the definition of "housing accommodation" shall not include "housing accommodations in buildings in which rentals are fixed by or subject to the supervision of the Commissioner of Housing." Such privately owned housing accommodations, commonly known as "Limited Dividend Housing," are subject to the regulatory control of the State Commissioner of Housing. Although 

Section 9 of the Rent and Eviction Regulations does not include such housing accommodations in the category of "Housing accommodations not subject to rent control" they are nevertheless not subject to control by the Regulations by reason of the specific provision of the Act. The 262

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

complaint filed with the Local Rent Office will, therefore, be rejected. 

§ 9(1)-Public Housing Authority. Administrator's Opinion No. 32. 

The Housing Authority of a city acquired title to certain lands on which there are dwellings occupied by over a hundred tenants. The Housing Authority proposes to demolish these accommodations for the purpose of constructing a Housing Authority project, construction to begin as soon as possible. Must the Housing Authority comply with the eviction sections of the Regulations?

No. Section 9(1) specifically states that the Rent and 

Eviction Regulations do not apply to housing accommodations owned or operated by a public housing authority. 

§ 9(8) Meaning of "Non-Housekeeping Accommodations." Administrator's Opinion No. 70. 

The tenant of a six-room one-family housing accommodation rents two furnished bedrooms to two paying subtenants who are not members of his immediate family. 

The remaining portion of the housing accommodation is occupied by the tenant as his residence. The sub-tenants. use the kitchen in common with the tenant. Are the accommodations so rented to the two sub-tenants considered "non-housekeeping furnished housing accommodations" and therefore not controlled within the meaning of Section 9(8) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations?

Yes. Non-housekeeping housing accommodations are those which do not contain separate kitchen and other housekeeping facilities. The bedrooms rented to the subtenants do not contain any cooking facilities. The fact that the sub-tenants have the privilege of using the kitchen in common with the tenant does not destroy the "nonhousekeeping" status of the accommodations. Similarly, 263

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

if the sub-tenant is granted the privilege of sharing or using other parts of the housing accommodation, such as a living room or dining room, in common with the tenant, the status of the accommodations as "non-housekeeping" is not destroyed.

On the other hand, where a tenant rents the entire housing accommodation to one sub-tenant with the exception of a room which is reserved by the tenant for his own occupancy, the housing accommodation rented to the sub-tenant would definitely be considered as housekeeping housing accommodations and would therefore not fall within the pur view of Section 9(8) of the Regulations. 

The same considerations apply to the renting of rooms in a house by the owner who occupies the same as his dwelling. 

§ 9(10) Entire Structure Containing 25 Rooms and Apartment Occupied by Lessee. Administrator's Opinion No. 43. 

A structure containing 29 rooms consisting of 25 rooms registered with O.H.E. and a four-room apartment is leased to a person who rents out the 25 rooms and occupies the apartment as his own dwelling. The rental reserved in the lease refers to the entire premises. The premises have always been used in that manner. Is the underlying lease subject to the Rent and Eviction Regulations?

Since not more than 25 rooms are rented or offered for rent by the lessee of the structure, the underlying lease does not fall within the exemption of Section 9(10) and is, therefore, subject to the Regulations. Rooms which are shared in common by all the roomers such as a kitchen or community room are not considered in computing the number of rooms offered for rent within the meaning of Section 9(10). 264

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS § 9(10) Nursing and Convalescent Home. Administrator's Opinion No. 

44. 

An owner of a house intends to lease out the premises as a nursing and convalescent home. The lessee intends to accommodate individuals who are aged, invalids, or convalescents. The services will include private room, furniture, all utilities, and general nursing care. 

In some instances the patients will be entirely bedridden, hand fed and require an attendant to carry them from place to place. 

Other patients will have a registered nurse in attendance on a 24-hour basis while others will have a nurse only during the daytime. Although no doctor will be stationed at the home, a house physician or physicians will be available on a 24-hour basis. In all cases a minimum of three meals per day will be served to each patient except where dietary requirements are otherwise prescribed. In the main, bathroom facilities will be shared. 

It is the intent of the operator to conduct the business for a profit. No surgical room, as such, will be available. Are the underlying lease and the individual rooms considered controlled housing accommodations?

No. The individual rooms are not housing accommodations within the meaning of that term as defined by Section 

2(2) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations. Therefore, the underlying lease of the structure is exempted from rent control under Section 9(10) of the Regulations. 

§ 9 (12)-Rental of Vacancies in One- or Two-Family Houses After April 1, 1953. Administrator's Opinion No. 103. 

A one-family house or an apartment in a two-family house becomes vacant on or after April 1, 1953. The landlord thereafter rents the vacant unit to two separate families, each consisting of a husband and wife, who are to share the kitchen and toilet facilities. Under these cir- 265

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

cumstances, may it be considered that the housing accommodations so rented are decontrolled within the meaning of Section 9(12) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations? 

No.

The exemption from rent control conferred under 

Section 2(2)(i) of the Emergency Housing Rent Control Law and Section 9(12) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations does not apply to the renting of housing accommodations to more than one family unit. It was not the Legislative intent to permit decontrol where the vacant apartment is subsequently rented to more than one family with the inevitable threat to health and safety caused by overcrowding and generally unsanitary and unhealthy conditions. 

The same considerations would apply to the rental of an apartment which was owner-occupied for a period of two years and became vacant on or after April 1, 1953 

[Emergency Housing Rent Control Law, Section 2(2)(h); 

Rent and Eviction Regulations, Section 9(11)]. 

§ 9(12)—Meaning of "vacant.” Administrator's Opinion No. 104. 

A one-family house is purchased for self-occupancy. The buyer, after having obtained title to the house, filed an application with the Local Rent Office for a certificate of eviction. After issuance of the order granting the certificate, and prior to the commencement of Court proceedings based upon the certificate, the tenant removed from the premises. Instead of taking possession of the vacant housing accommodation, the landlord re-rented it to another tenant. Does the removal of the original tenant from the housing accommodations cause the housing accommodations to become decontrolled?

No. The exemption from rent control conferred under 

Section 2(2)(i) of the Emergency Housing Rent Control Law and Section 9(12) of the Rent and Eviction Regula tions contemplates a situation where a tenant voluntarily 266

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

vacates controlled housing accommodations in a one- or two-family house. It was not the Legislative intent to decontrol housing accommodations which become vacant where the Commission grants the application of a landlord for a certificate of eviction to permit the landlord to obtain possession and the landlord fails to occupy the premises. 

In such a situation, the removal of the tenant from the housing accommodations cannot be considered to be voluntary even though Court proceedings to recover possession have not yet been commenced. 

§ 9(12)-One-Family Home Converted to Legal Rooming House After April 1, 1953. Administrator's Opinion No. 107. 

A one-family house becomes vacant on or after April 1, 1953. The owner thereafter converts the house into a legal rooming house, receives a certificate of occupancy for such use from the local authorities, and rents the rooms therein to various tenants. Are the rooms so rented decontrolled because of Section 9(12) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations?

No. The exemption from rent control conferred under 

Section 2(2)(i) of the Emergency Housing Rent Control Law and Section 9(12) of the Rent and Eviction Regula tions does not apply to the renting of housing accomodations to more than one family unit. It was not the Legislative intent to permit decontrol to a one-family house which is converted into a rooming house since, inevitably, overcrowding arises which is a threat to health and safety. 

The same considerations will apply to the conversion into a legal rooming house of an apartment which had been owner occupied for a period of two years and had become vacant on or after April 1, 1953. [Emergency Housing Rent Control Law, Section 2(2)(h); Rent and Eviction Regulations, Section 9(11).] 

267

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS $9(13) The Effect of the Sale of a Two-Family House in Nassau County on or After July 1, 1955 to a Purchaser Who Subsequently Occupies 

One of the Apartments in the House. Administrator's Opinion No. 

111 (For Erie, Monroe, Nassau, Schenectady, Oneida and Onondaga 

Counties only).

On July 1, 1955 each apartment in a two-family house situated in Nassau County was occupied by a tenant. On 

July 15, 1955 one of the tenants vacated his apartment and the landlord thereafter moved into this vacant apartment. Is the tenant-occupied apartment exempt from rent control pursuant to the provisions of Section 2(2)(j) of the Emergency Housing Rent Control Law and Section 

9(13) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations?

The owner of the two-family house must actually be living in one of the apartments on July 1, 1955 in order to effect the decontrol of the other apartment which is tenantoccupied. This is consistent with the Legislative intent to prevent exactions of unjust, unreasonable and oppressive rents and rental agreements and to forestall profiteering, speculation and other disruptive practices. Since the owner first took occupancy after July 1, 1955, the structure was not completely decontrolled and the remaining tenant continues to have the protection afforded by the Act and the Regulations.

Where, however, one of the apartments was actually owner-occupied on July 1, 1955 and the owner thereafter sells the house to another who moves into the apartment previously occupied by the former owner the tenant-occupied apartment continues to remain decontrolled. 

The same considerations will apply where the two-family house is located in Erie, Monroe, Schenectady, Oneida and 

Onondaga Counties. [Emergency Housing Rent Control 

Law, Sections 2(2)(j) and 12(3); Rent and Eviction Reg ulations, Sections 9(14) and 9(15)]. 

268

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS § 9 (14)—Decontrol of Housing Accommodations in Onondaga County Containing Four Rental Units or Less. Administrator's Opinion No. 116 

(For Onondaga County only).

A landlord who owns property in Onondaga County consisting of a store and four apartments in the upper portion of the building requests information concerning the control status of the apartments after July 1, 1957 under the new law. Section 2(2)(j) of the Emergency Housing Rent 

Control Law was amended by Chapter 755, Laws of 1957, to provide that on and after July 1, 1957 any housing accommodations in Onondaga County containing four rental units or less shall become and remain decontrolled. This provision of the Act was incorporated into Section 9(14) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations. 

The Administrator interprets the term, "four rental units or less," to mean four or less residential units without any commercial space in the structure. Therefore, in the factual situation presented by this landlord, the residential units would continue to remain under control since the structure also contains a store.

This new provision includes one- and two-family houses as well as larger structures containing no more than four rental units. The Administrator has held that a one-family and a two-family house could not be a building which was not used exclusively for residential purposes and that therefore these buildings remained under control. This interpretation has been sustained by the Courts in the Matter of Present (McGoldrick) [279 App Div 1010, 111 NYS 2d 

610] and Wisotsky v McGoldrick [279 App Div 1011, 111 

NYS2d 609, affd no opinion 304 NY 619]. During the past year the Courts have also passed upon the Administrator's interpretation relating to vacancy decontrol and have approved his determination that a vacancy in a so-called two-family house which was partially rented for commercial 269

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

use did not result in the decontrol of the vacant apartment. [Reiss v Weaver, Sup Ct NY Co, NYLJ August 6, 1956, p 3, col 6; Croccitto v Weaver, Sup Ct, Kings Co, NYLJ August 

7, 1956, p 3, col 7; 351 Gramatan Corp. v Weaver, Sup Ct, Westchester Co, NYLJ August 24, 1956, p 6, col 8].

In enacting this amendment to Section 2(2)(j) of the Act the Legislature must be deemed to be cognizant of this line of Court decision, as well as the Administrator's interpretations, and it must have had them in mind when it found it no longer necessary to control buildings in Onondaga County containing no more than four rental units. There is nothing in the Act which expresses an intent to give special treatment to landlords of one- and two-family houses in Onondaga County. In the absence of such expression of intent, the Administrator is of the opinion that the Legislature intended to award decontrol only to landlords of small buildings without commercial space and containing no more than four residential rental units, thereby extending decontrol from one- and two-family structures to four-family structures containing only residential units.

The 

A similar interpretation has been applied to another section of the Act-subdivision 2 of Section 4(4)(a), which provides for increases in maximum rent to a landlord who owns no more than four rental units within the State. 

Administrator has for many years interpreted this requirement to give relief only to owners of no more than four residential rental units located in a structure or structures which do not have any commercial space. This interpretation has not been reversed by the courts. 270

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS Section 10. Housing accommodations subject to rent con trol, but exempted from control by these Regulations.

§ 10(2)—Payment of Rent by Janitor. Administrator's Opinion No. 42. 

The landlord is an elderly woman residing on the first floor of a two-family house. An agreement in writing was entered into between the landlord and the occupant of the second floor apartment. The agreement provides that the second floor occupant is to pay the landlord $15.00 per month and in addition, is to pay for and provide heat and hot water and make all necessary repairs for the entire house. Is the second floor apartment subject to Rent and 

Eviction Regulations?

Yes. Section 10(2) of the Regulations exempts from rent control "dwelling space occupied by domestic servants, superintendents, caretakers, managers, or other employees to whom the space is provided as part or all of their compensation without payment of rent and who are employed for the purpose of rendering services in connection with the premises of which the dwelling space is a part." Since the occupant of the second floor apartment pays $15.00 per month to the landlord, the same constitutes a payment of rent and the occupant is considered a tenant within the meaning of Section 2(7) of the Regulations. The dwelling space occupied by the tenant is, therefore, not exempted from control by Section 10 (2) of the Regulations. 

§ 10 (3)-Change in Rental Practice As Affecting Seasonal Exemption. 

Administrator's Opinion No. 101. 

Prior to October 1, 1945, an apartment in a resort area customarily was rented for the summer season only (June 

1st-September 30th) at $200 per month. It had not been rented during the other months of the year and had not been rented from November 1, 1943 to October 29, 1944.

Thereafter it was rented on a twelve-month basis at $25 271

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

per week. Has the landlord forfeited his right to seasonal exemption from control for the period June 1st-September 

30th ?

Yes. The purpose of the exemption granted by the Commission under Section 10(3) of the Regulations was to exempt summer seasonal accommodations from control during the summer season (June 1 through September 30) because such accommodations when used solely for summer seasonal accommodations do not form part of the normal housing supply in those areas where housing accommodations are subject to rent control. The factors peculiar to housing accommodations which are purely and exclusively seasonal in character were the basis upon which such an exemption was previously granted by the Federal rent regulations and was continued under the State regulations. 

The exemption under Section 10(3) presupposed con tinuance of the seasonal character of the renting. A housing accommodation is entitled to the exemption only where: (a) it is rented for a short seasonal period at a seasonal rental, or where (b) besides being rented for a short seasonal period at a seasonal rental, it is also rented for the remainder of the year or a part thereof at a rental much lower than the seasonal rental. A landlord who has taken advantage of the year-round demand and has made the property part of the normal supply of housing in that area at the same weekly or monthly rental during both the winter and summer months, must be deemed to have abandoned his former practice of renting on a seasonal basis. 

An order may be issued under Section 36 determining that the property is under control for all twelve months and establishing a monthly rental on a comparable basis, taking into consideration all the equities involved. 272

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS Section 13. Commercial or professional renting of con trolled housing accommodations on or after May 1, 1955. 

§ 13—Combined Business and Dwelling Uses Where Premises Are Rented to a Single Tenant. See Administrator's Opinion No. 115 Under Section 2(2). 

Section 15. Waiver of benefit void. 

§ 15-Cancellation clauses in Voluntary Leases. See Administrator's Opinion No. 74 Under Section 33(2). 

PART II-MAXIMUM RENTS Section 21. Maximum rents for housing accommodations, except in hotels. 

§ 21 (1) Application of Section 13-a-2(c)-Emergency Housing Rent Control Law. Administrator's Opinion No. 39 (For New York City only) [Revoked]. 

§§ 21(1) and 21(2)—Retroactive Order Issued by O.H.E. After March 1, 1950. Administrator's Opinion No. 8. 

A housing accommodation had a maximum rent of $50.00 per month. Subsequent to March 1, 1950, the Office of the 

Housing Expediter issued an order increasing the maximum rent to $55.00 per month, retroactive to January 10, 1950. What is the maximum rent?

Under Sections 21(1) and 21(2) of the Regulations, the maximum rent is $50.00 per month. It would make no difference if the tenant paid the retroactive increase since the rent actually prescribed on March 1, 1950 was $50.00 per month. 

§§ 21 (1) and 21(2)—Payment by Tenant of More Than Maximum Rent on March 1, 1950 for Additional Services or Facilities. Administrator's Opinion No. 9. 

A housing accommodation has a maximum rent of $40.00 per month. Prior to March 1, 1950, at the request of the tenant, the landlord added additional equipment consisting 

—18 

273

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

of a mechanical refrigerator, and filed a petition for a rent increase with the Office of the Housing Expediter. The 

O.H.E. issued an order on March 20, 1950, increasing the maximum rent from $40.00 per month to $43.00 per month, retroactive to February 15, 1950. The tenant did not wait for the order increasing the maximum rent but began paying the additional $3.00 per month immediately so that on 

March 1, 1950 the landlord actually collected from the tenant the sum of $43.00 per month. What is the maximum rent?

Under Sections 21(1) and 21(2) of the Regulations, the maximum rent is $40.00 per month as that was the rent prescribed by the Federal law on that date. The fact that the landlord collected $43.00 on that date does not alter the result. 

§§ 21(1) and 21(2)—Effect of Order Issued Prior to March 1, 1950 Reducing Rent of Unit Recontrolled by Federal Law. Administrator's Opinion No. 10. 

Housing accommodations were owner-occupied and were first rented in August, 1947 for the sum of $100.00 per month, at which time they were not subject to Federal control. On April 1, 1949, the unit was controlled by the 

Federal law at $100.00 per month. On December 4, 1949, the Office of the Housing Expediter issued an order reducing the maximum rent to $60.00 per month. What is the maximum rent?

Under Sections 21(1) and 21(2) of the Regulations, the maximum rent is $60.00 per month, the rent prescribed by 

Federal law on March 1, 1950. 

§§ 21(1) and 21(2)—Alternate Order Based Upon Additional Occupants. 

Administrator's Opinion No. 12. 

A housing accommodation had a maximum rent of $50.00 per month. Prior to March 1, 1950, an order was issued 

274

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

by the Office of the Housing Expediter which permitted the landlord to charge an additional $5.00 per month because of one additional sub-tenant and $2.50 per month for each additional sub-tenant. What is the effect of this order under the Regulations?

An adjustment based upon an increase in sub-tenants or in occupants is considered to be an adjustment granted because of an increase in the service or facilities provided with the housing accommodation. Under Sections 21(1) and 21 

(2) of the Regulations, the varying rents established by the order remain in effect. Just so long as the additional sub-tenant or additional occupants remain in possession, the landlord may collect whatever increase was granted by the terms of the order. Should the additional sub-tenant or the additional occupants vacate, the landlord ceases to have the right to collect the increase granted because of their presence and may only collect the lower amount. 

Should there be a dispute between the parties as to whether the additional sub-tenant or the additional occupants are still in possession, the local court may determine this factual question. 

§§ 21(1) and 21(2)—Order Establishing Seasonal Rents. Administrator's Opinion No. 13. 

Prior to March 1, 1950 an order was issued by the Office of the Housing Expediter fixing different seasonal rents for summer and winter occupancy. The order provided that the maximum rent from October 1 until May 30 of each year was $25.00 per month and that the maximum rent from June 1, to September 30 of each year was $200.00 per month. What is the effect of this order under the Regulations?

Under Sections 21(1) and 21(2) of the Regulations, the varying rents established by the order remain in effect.

275

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

The maximum rent of the housing accommodations is $25.00 per month from October 1 until May 30 and $200.00 per month from June 1 to September 30 of each year. 

§§ 21(1) and 21(2)-Different Rates Registered on March 1, 1950. Administrator's Opinion No. 14. 

On March 1, 1950, the registration statement of a rooming house filed with the Office of the Housing Expediter established a maximum rent for single occupancy of $8.00 per week and $25.00 for monthly occupancy of apartment or room. On March 1, 1950, the room was rented on a monthly basis for single occupancy. What is the maximum rent and is there any necessity for the landlord to file a new registration?

Under Sections 21(1) and 21(2) of the Regulations, the maximum rent for single occupancy on a weekly basis is $8.00 per week and $25.00 on a monthly basis. There is no necessity for the landlord filing a new registration statement. 

§ 21(2)——Alternate Orders With and Without Service of Painting. Administrator's Opinion No. 11 (For New York City only). 

A housing accommodation had a maximum rent of $50.00 per month. Prior to March 1, 1950 an order was issued by the Office of the Housing Expediter reducing the maximum rent from $50 to $45.00 per month because of the failure of the landlord to paint. The order contained a provision that upon restoration of the service by the landlord, the reduced rent would automatically revert to $50.00 per month without further petition or order by the Rent Director. What is the effect of this order under the Regulations?

Under Section 21(2) of the Regulations, the alternate maximum rents established by the order remain in effect. 

If the landlord has restored, or subsequently restores the 276

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

service of painting, he may collect the original maximum rent of $50.00 per month without application to the Local 

Rent Administrator. 

The tenant may not unreasonably refuse to permit the landlord to resume his practice of periodically painting the housing accommodation or unreasonably refuse to accept the restoration of the customary service where the established time period for painting has expired and the housing accommodation has not been painted either by the landlord or tenant within this time period.

However, should there be a dispute between landlord and tenant as to whether the service has been restored, or whether the landlord has in good faith offered to restore the service, the factual question involved must ultimately be determined by the courts. 

§ 21(2)—Retroactive Orders Issued by O.H.E. After March 1, 1949. Administrator's Opinion No. 17 (For New York City only). 

On March 1, 1949, a housing accommodation within the

City of New York had a maximum rent of $50.00 per month. 

On November 12, 1949, the Office of the Housing Expediter increased the maximum rent to $60.00 per month on the basis of comparability. This order was retroactive to January 10, 1949 and the tenant paid the retroactive increase. 

What is the maximum rent and what effect has the payment by the tenant of the retroactive increase?

Under Section 21(2) of the Regulations, the maximum rent is $50.00 per month. It makes no difference that the tenant paid the retroactive increase.

The maximum rent is $60.00 per month. A major capital improvement under the Federal Regulations is considered as "new or additional services or facilities to which the tenant then in possession had agreed, either expressly or impliedly" within the purview of Section 21(2) of the

§ 21(2)—Order of O.H.E. Granting Rent Free Period. Administrator's Opinion No. 45 (For New York City only).

with the tenant entitled to a rent free period from March

§ 21(2)-15 Percent Leases Under O.H.E. Administrator's Opinion No. 

46 (For New York City only). 

On July 1, 1947, a housing accommodation within the

City of New York had a maximum rent of $100.00 per month pursuant to Federal law. The landlord and tenant entered into a voluntary written agreement to increase the maximum rent by 15 percent to $115.00 per month. 

The landlord failed to file the lease with the Office of the Housing Expediter as required by the Federal law and the maximum rent, therefore, remained at $100.00 per month. 

However, the tenant began paying the increased rent immediately. Subsequently, the landlord filed a petition with the Office of the Housing Expediter to increase the maximum rent from $100.00 per month to $115.00 per month on the ground that he had inadvertently failed to file the lease and the Office of the Housing Expediter issued an order prior to March 1, 1950 increasing the maximum rent accordingly. What is the maximum rent?

Under Section 21(2) of the Regulations, the maximum rent is $115.00 per month. The granting of the lease by the landlord may be considered as an additional service and benefit to the tenant within the contemplation of the Regulations.

The result would be different if the order had been issued 280

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

after March 1, 1950, although effective prior to that date.

In such a case, the maximum rent under Section 21(2) of the Regulations would be $100.00 per month, the rent actually prescribed by Federal law on March 1, 1950.

Section 23. Maximum rents for housing accommodations, including equalization adjustment effective May 1, 1953. 

§ 23-Equalization Adjustment As Affecting Voluntary Concessions. Administrator's Opinion No. 87 (For New York City only). See Administrator's Opinion No. 18 Under Section 21(2). 

An apartment in the City of New York had a maximum rent of $60.00 per month on March 1, 1950. From the inception of his tenancy in 1942, the tenant had always received a concession of free rent during June, although no order confirming it was issued by the Federal authorities. 

The landlord now seeks to increase the rent to $69.00 per month beginning May 1, 1953. What is the maximum rent under the Equalization Adjustment on May 1, 1953?

Regulations, the maximum rent effective May 1, 1953 is $69.00 per month. Should the tenant claim that his rental agreement entitles him to free rent during the month of 

June each year, the local courts have jurisdiction to decide that question. 

§ 23—Computation of Equalization Adjustment Where Concession Order Was Issued by Federal Authorities. Administrator's Opinion No. 88 

(For New York City only).

An apartment in the City of New York had a maximum rent of $60.00 per month on March 1, 1943. On June 1, 1946, 281

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

the Federal agency issued an order granting free rent during the month of July of each year as a concession. The landlord has demanded that the tenant pay a monthly rent of $69.00 as of May 1, 1953. What is the new maximum rent under the Equalization Adjustment as of May 1, 1953? 

Regulations, the maximum rent effective May 1, 1953 is $69.00 per month. However, the provision of the order issued by the Federal authorities granting to the tenant the month of July during each year of occupancy as a rentfree concession period is not changed by the Equalization Adjustment provision. The tenant will continue to receive his July concession period. 

§ 23—Computation of Equalization Adjustment Where Maximum · Rent Was Reduced by Federal Authorities Due to Concession. Administrator's Opinion No. 89 (For New York City only). 

An apartment in the City of New York had a maximum rent of $60.00 per month on March 1, 1943. On June 1, 1946 the Federal agency reduced the maximum rent to $55.00 per month. The reduction was based on a one-month concession period under the rental agreement in effect on March 1, 1943. The landlord has demanded that the tenant pay a monthly rent of $69.00 as of May 1, 1953. What is the new maximum rent under the Equalization Adjustment as of May 1, 1953?

Under Section 4(3-a) of the Act and Section 23 of the 

Regulations, the maximum rent effective May 1, 1953 is $55.00 plus 15 percent, or $63.25 per month. The Act specifically provides that "Nothing contained in this section, however, shall have the effect of increasing the maximum rent of any housing accommodation more than fifteen percentum above the maximum rent in effect on April thirtieth, nineteen hundred fifty-three." 282

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS § 23-Computation of Equalization Adjustment Where Statutory Lease Provides for Additional Services or Equipment. Administrator's Opinion No. 90. 

The maximum rent for an apartment was $40.00 per month. On March 15, 1951 the landlord and tenant entered into a lease which complies with all of the requirements of Section 33(2) of the Regulations. The lease rent was $46.00 per month. As a consideration for the execution of the lease the landlord agreed to replace the mechanical refrigerator. This agreement was embodied in the lease. 

The landlord now seeks to increase the rent to $52.00 per month beginning May 1, 1953, claiming that the previous increase was for additional equipment and that he was entitled to the 15% Equalization Adjustment provided for in the Act. What is the maximum rent under the Equalization Adjustment on May 1, 1953?

Regulations, the maximum rent on May 1, 1953 remains at $46.00 per month. The amount of increases because of increase in services, etc., may only be added where the increase in rent was authorized by order. In the instant situation the increase was obtained by means of a statutory lease. Under these circumstances the application of the 

Equalization Adjustment does not result in any change in the maximum rent. 

§ 23—Computation of Equalization Adjustment Where Maximum Rent Was Increased Due to Change from Submetering Electric Current to a Rent Inclusion Plan. Administrator's Opinion No. 91. 

On March 1, 1943 the maximum rent for an apartment was $60.00 per month. On that date the landlord submetered the electrical current and charged the tenant for such service on the basis of the tenant's actual consumption. 

The landlord applied to the Local Rent Administrator for permission to adopt a rent inclusion plan with the 283

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

tenants of the building. On September 15, 1952 the Local 

Rent Administrator issued an order fixing the maximum rent for an apartment at $62.75 per month including unlimited electrical current. The landlord has demanded that the tenant pay a monthly rent of $72.16 commencing May 1, 1953. What is the new maximum rent under the Equalization Adjustment?

Regulations, the maximum rent on May 1, 1953 is $60.00 plus 15% ($9.00) plus $2.75, or $71.75 per month. The amount of the increase for unlimited electrical current is an increase in services authorized by order of the Administrator and is added after applying the Equalization Adjustment to the basic March 1, 1943 rent.

4. An apartment in the City of New York had a maximum rent of $60.00 per month on March 1, 1943. On June 1, 1946 the Federal agency reduced the maximum rent to $55.00 per month based upon the existence of a one-month concession period in the rental agreement in effect on 

March 1, 1943. On September 15, 1948 the Federal agency increased the maximum rent to $70.00 per month on the basis of the $55.00 rent being substantially lower than comparable rents. The landlord has demanded that the tenant pay a monthly rent of $80.50 as of May 1, 1953. What is the new maximum rent under the Equalization Adjustment provided for in the new Act as of May 1, 1953?

There would be no change in the maximum rent since the Equalization Adjustment would bring the March 1, 1943 rent to less than the present maximum rent. The maximum rent would therefore remain at $70.00 per month. 

5. An apartment in the City of New York had a maximum rent of $60.00 per month on March 1, 1943. On June 

1, 1946 the Federal agency reduced the maximum rent to $55.00 per month based upon the existence of a one-month concession period in the rental agreement in effect on 

March 1, 1943. On September 15, 1948 the Federal agency increased the maximum rent to $60.00 per month on the basis of the $55.00 rent being substantially lower than comparable rents. On March 15, 1951 the landlord and tenant entered into a voluntary lease which complied with all of the requirements of Section 33 (2) of the State Regulations. 

The lease provided for a monthly rental of $66.00, an increase of only 10%. The lease has now expired and the tenant requests advice as to the rent he may be required to pay on May 1, 1953 under the Equalization Adjustment provision of the new Act.

Regulations, the new maximum rent in effect on May 1, 286

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

1953 is $69.00 per month. Since the landlord has already received a 10% increase by lease he may now only receive an additional 5%, which amounts to $3.00 per month above the $66.00 previously in effect. 

6. An apartment in the City of New York had a maximum rent of $60.00 per month on March 1, 1943. On June 1, 1946 the Federal agency reduced the maximum rent to $55.00 per month based upon the existence of a one-month concession period in the rental agreement in effect on

The lease provided for a monthly rental of $69.00, an increase of 15%. The lease has now expired and the tenant requests advice as to the rent he may be required to pay on May 1, 1953 under the Equalization Adjustment provision of the new Act.

The rent on May 1, 1953 would remain at $69.00 per month because the maximum rent has already been increased 15% above the March 1, 1943 rent.

§ 23—Computation of Equalization Adjustment Where There Have Been Seasonal Rents in Effect. Administrator's Opinion No. 96. 

Landlords and tenants of controlled properties located in a summer resort community have requested advice as to the effect of the equalization adjustment upon controlled seasonal rents. The following appeared to be the most frequent situations: 

1. On March 1, 1943 an apartment situated in a summer resort area was rented for $40 per month for the winter months from October 1st to May 31st and it was so registered. Commencing with June 1, 1943, it was rented 

—19 

289

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

for the summer months, June through September, for a total of $600 or $150 per month. The practice of two different levels of rent, a low one for the winter months and a much higher one for the summer months, was a regular custom in this community.

Thereafter, the Federal authorities issued an order establishing two levels of rent for these accommodations, to wit: $40 per month for the period from October 1st to May 31st and $150 per month for the period from June 1st to September 30th. Does the equalization adjustment apply to either or both of these rentals?

Regulations, the equalization adjustment applies to both of these rents so established under the Federal regulations then in effect. Since the apartment had a maximum rent on March 1, 1943 in the sum of $40 per month, the equalization adjustment obviously applies to that rental. However, the apartment did not have a maximum rent in effect on 

March 1, 1943 for the summer period. Therefore the first rent of $150 established for the summer season on June 1, 1943 was a maximum rent "first established pursuant to the Federal Act prior to July 1, 1947." The equalization adjustment would therefore also apply to this summer season maximum rent. 

2. Assume the same facts as set forth in paragraph "1" except that the order issued by the Federal authorities reduced the rents charged by the landlord and established them in the sum of $25 per month for the period from 

October 1 to May 31 and $125 per month for the period from 

June 1 to September 30. These are the maximum rents which the landlord was permitted to collect as of April 30, 1953.

In accordance with the opinion expressed in number 1, 

290

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

the equalization adjustment would apply to both of the maximum rents so established. However, since that section of the Act specifically provides that the equalization adjustment shall not increase the maximum rent of any housing accommodation more than 15% above the maximum rent in effect on April 30, 1953, the computation of 15% will be made upon the basis of the maximum rents as previously established by the order of the Federal authorities: to wit, $25 during the winter months and $125 during the summer months. 

3. Assume the same facts as set forth in paragraph “1” except that the apartment had not been rented during the winter months and there was, therefore, no registered rent as of March 1, 1943. The apartment was first rented on June 1, 1943 at $150 per month for the summer months. 

Thereafter an order was issued by the Federal authorities establishing two maximum rents for the apartment: $40 per month for the winter months, and $150 per month for the summer months.

The result in this case is similar to the result in number 1 above and the equalization adjustment would apply to each of the maximum rents so established by the Federal authorities. 

4. Assume the same facts as set forth in number 1 except that the apartment was vacant on March 1, 1943. It was first rented thereafter on May 1, 1943 for $1200 per annum payable at the rate of $100 per month. Thereafter an order was issued by the Federal authorities establishing two levels: $40 per month for the period from October 1st to 

May 31st and $150 per month for the period from June 1st to September 30th.

The equalization adjustment would apply to each of these two maximum rents. 291

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

§ 23--Equalization Adjustment Where Housing Accommodations Had 

Been Rented Unfurnished and Where Thereafter First Rented as 

Fully Furnished Accommodations Prior to July 1, 1947, Pursuant to Federal Law. Administrator's Opinion No. 97. 

1. An unfurnished apartment had a maximum rent of $40 per month on March 1, 1943. Prior to July 1, 1947 it became vacant and the landlord rented the apartment, fully furnished, for the sum of $60 per month. The landlord filed a registration statement of this first rental as required under the Federal law on that date. On August 15, 1947 the Federal authorities issued an order approving this maximum rent of $60 per month. On April 30, 1953 the maximum rent for the furnished apartment was still $60 per month, the rent then being paid by the tenant. What is the new maximum rent under the equalization adjustment as of May 1, 1953?

Under the Federal law in effect prior to July 1, 1947, change from an unfurnished to a fully furnished housing accommodation established a new maximum rent. Since the apartment did not have a registered furnished maximum rent on March 1, 1943, this maximum rent as a completely furnished unit established after March 1, 1943 was "the maximum rent first established pursuant to the 

Federal Act prior to July 1, 1947." It therefore follows that on April 30, 1953 there were varying maximum rents previously registered under the Federal Act for this apartment, dependent upon whether the apartment was rented unfurnished or fully furnished. 

The equalization adjustment of 15% will therefore apply to both of these maximum rents." 

2. Assume the same facts as set forth in paragraph "1" except that instead of approving this first rental of $60 as a fully furnished unit, the order issued by the Federal authorities reduced this rental to $50 per month. On April 2922

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

30, 1953 this apartment had two maximum rents: one of $40 when the apartment was rented unfurnished, and the other $50 when it was rented fully furnished. 

The equalization adjustment would apply to both of these maximum rents. However, if the apartment is rented fully furnished, the equalization adjustment of 15% would apply to the $50 maximum rent because of the limitation that the equalization adjustment shall not increase any maximum rent more than 15% above the maximum rent in effect on April 30, 1953.

§ 24-Date of Painting as Determining Obligation to Redecorate. Administrator's Opinion No. 56 (For New York City only). 

A landlord had a practice of painting a particular apartment every two years. He painted the apartment two years ago. The tenant now in possession moved into this apartment a year ago and at that time paid for his own painting. 

The tenant now claims that the two year interval has run and that the landlord is now obligated to paint and decorate. The landlord had advised the tenant that since the apartment was painted only a year ago, he will not paint for another year, and the tenant has therefore filed an application for a rent reduction based upon the landlord's failure to paint. What disposition shall be made of this application?

The application should be accepted for processing. In computing the time period, the date when the landlord last painted the apartment determines whether or not he is in default of his obligation to maintain the painting and decorating service. 293

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

§ 24-Removal of Television Antenna by Landlord Where Rent on March 1, 1950 Included Privilege of Maintaining Same. Administrator's Opinion No. 65. 

A tenant installed a television antenna on the roof of the building with the consent of the landlord. The landlord filed a petition with the Office of the Housing Expediter and received an order, prior to March 1, 1950, increasing the maximum rent by the sum of $1.00 based upon the additional service of the use of the roof for the television antenna. This order has never been modified or revoked. 

The landlord has now notified the tenant that he must remove the television antenna from the roof. The tenant requests advice as to his rights under the Rent and Eviction Regulations. 

Since on March 1, 1950, the date determining the maximum rent, the use of the roof by the tenant for a television antenna was a service provided by the landlord with the housing accommodation, within the meaning of Section 24 (formerly Section 23) of the Regulations, the landlord is under the obligation to continue providing this service. 

The landlord may not demand the removal nor remove the television antenna from the roof without first obtaining an order under Section 35(1) permitting such removal. The removal by the landlord without such permissive order would be a violation of the Act and the Regulations. 

The tenant would have the same right where he had given an increase in the maximum rent by lease, and the permission to erect or maintain the television antenna was specifically included in the lease. In this situation, also, the right to maintain a television antenna upon the premises of the landlord has become a service to which the tenant is entitled under the new maximum rent established by the lease. 294

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

E 

§ 24-Removal of Television Antenna by Landlord Where Privilege Was Granted as Inducement for Increase-Lease Under O.H.E. Administrator's Opinion No. 66 [Revoked]. 

§ 24-Removal of Television Antenna by Landlord Where Installed Prior to March 1, 1950. Administrator's Opinion No. 75. 

Prior to March 1, 1950 a tenant installed a television antenna on the roof of the building. The landlord did not receive an increase in the maximum rent based upon this installation by way of an order of the Housing Expediter, nor did the tenant install the antenna as a consideration for the execution of a lease under Section 204(b) 

of the Federal Housing and Rent Act. The landlord has now notified the tenant that he must remove the television antenna from the roof or agree to enter into a lease to increase the maximum rent by 15 percent pursuant to Section 33(2) of the Regulations. The tenant requests advice as to his rights under the Rent and Eviction Regulations. 

The landlord may not demand of the tenant that he remove the television antenna, nor may he interfere with the tenant's television antenna, since on March 1, 1950, the date determining the maximum rent, the use of the roof by the tenant for a television antenna was an essential service provided with the housing accommodations, within the meaning of Section 24 (formerly Section 23) of the Reg ulations.

However, since the maximum rent in effect on March 1, 1950 did not include any allowance for this extra service, the landlord may now file an application pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Regulations and he will be entitled to receive an adjustment not to exceed the sum of $2.00 per month. 295

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS § 24-Change from Manually-Operated Elevators to Automatic Elevators.

§ 24-Services Required to be Provided on March 1, 1950. Administrator's Opinion No. 98. 

The date which determines the maximum rent, March 1, 1950, is the date which determines the services and equipment required to be provided by the landlord to the tenant under the State Regulations. The proper inquiry therefore in all these cases is whether there has been a decrease in essential services from those which were either actually provided on March 1, 1950, or which were required to be provided on March 1, 1950. It is not difficult to determine which essential services were actually provided on the freeze date, March 1, 1950. The difficulty arises when certain services were not provided on that date and the tenants now maintain that these were essential services which were required to be provided and which the landlord had wrongfully eliminated.

In determining this question as to the essential services. which were "required to be provided on March 1, 1950" but which were not provided on that date, and are still not provided, it becomes necessary to consider the respective rights of the parties under the rent control laws in effect on March 1, 1950, a date two months prior to the time when

State rent control took effect. On March 1, 1950 the federal government was in control of rents and evictions, and had jurisdiction to decrease maximum rents in those cases where it determined that there had been decreases in essential services which reduced the rental value of the housing accommodations. New York City also had certain local laws in effect on March 1, 1950 but these had very little bearing on the question of services. 

The proper inquiry, therefore, must be as to the nature of the essential services required to be provided under 296

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

the federal rent regulations as of March 1, 1950. The applicable test is whether the tenants would have been entitled to rent reductions based upon a decrease in essential services if they had filed applications prior to March 1, 1950, with the Office of the Housing Expediter. This, of necessity, involves a careful study of the factual situation in each case, and of the federal regulations and policies in effect on March 1, 1950. It may be added that these federal regulations and policies varied from time to time so that it is essential to study the chronological record in each case in order to ascertain the rights of landlords and tenants as of certain dates. 

It should be noted that while March 1, 1943 was the original maximum rent date or freeze date for New York

City, it does not necessarily follow that in every case the services the landlord was furnishing on March 1, 1943 were the services he remained obligated to furnish under federal law as of March 1, 1950. In some cases the landlord's obligation was less on March 1, 1950 than it was on March 1, 1943. For example, the maximum rent of housing accommodations might have been decreased prior to March 1, 1950 by order of the federal office because of the elimination by the landlord of certain services or equipment. In such cases the lower maximum rent on March 

1, 1950 would not have obligated the landlord to furnish a service previously made available to the tenant on March 

1, 1943. Conversely, certain services not furnished by the landlord to the tenants on March 1, 1943 might thereafter have become part of the services to which the tenant was entitled on March 1, 1950. For example, a tenant may have given to the landlord a lease pursuant to Section 204(b) of the Federal Act increasing the maximum rent and simultaneously obligating the landlord to furnish new and additional services. This obligation would remain in effect 297

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

on March 1, 1950 although the obligation to furnish this service was not present on March 1, 1943.

In determining whether or not a service is an essential one, it is relevant to examine into the attitude of the parties prior to March 1, 1950 in order to determine whether they themselves considered the services to be an essential one. 

Rent control experience has shown that where tenants are deprived of a service they deem essential, they do not usually suffer this deprivation without challenge. On the other hand, if the eliminated service is not considered essential, tenants, even if dissatisfied, do not file and press complaints with the appropriate authorities. If the tenants failed to press this claim before the federal rent agency this failure is a relevant factor in determining whether or not the decrease in services represented a decrease in an essential service.

§ 33(1)—Sub-Lease of Apartment, Furnished for the Summer Months. 

Administrator's Opinion No. 26. 

A tenant wishes to sub-lease his apartment with his own furniture and furnishings for the summer months only. 

The maximum rent of the apartment does not include furniture and furnishings. What procedure may the tenant follow in order to obtain an increase in the maximum rent for the furniture and furnishings upon sub-renting for the summer months?

The tenant is considered a landlord under the Rent and 298

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

Eviction Regulations when he sub-leases the apartment he occupies. The procedure to be followed is similar to that set forth in Administrator's Opinion No. 2, under

Section 33(1). 

The tenant may file an application pursuant to Section 

33(1) of the Regulations before he sub-leases the apartment. The application must set forth in detail the furniture and furnishings which are to be supplied to the subtenant. The Local Rent Administrator will then issue an order increasing the maximum rent by such amount as he finds to be the added rental value of the apartment by reason of the furniture and furnishings. The tenant may then sub-lease the apartment at the increased maximum rent. 

The order of the Local Rent Administrator will state that the tenant must continue to maintain the additional furniture and furnishings within the apartment during the period of the sub-letting unless permission to remove such furniture and furnishings is granted pursuant to 

Section 35(1) of the Regulations. (For the procedure re quired to evict the sub-tenant should he fail to vacate at the end of the agreed period of sub-letting, see Adminis trator's Opinion No. 28 under Section 54.) § 33(1)—Removal of Television Antenna by Landlord Where Installed prior to March 1, 1950.
