§ 33(1)-Improvements to Property Required by Multiple Residence Law. 

Administrator's Opinion No. 86. 

At the 1952 Session of the Legislature, the "Multiple

Residence Law" was amended so as to make it apply to all multiple dwellings having three or more families residing therein in all cities of the State of less than 500,000 popula- 299

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

tion and in all towns and villages. The owner of a threefamily house occupied by three rent-paying tenants has been served with a notice by the local authorities requiring him to do certain work in and about the building, so as to comply with the provisions of this law. The owner of the building has requested information whether the cost of such work can be the basis for increases in the maximum rents paid by the tenants. 

Any work done by a landlord in and about a building occupied by tenants so as to comply with the requirements of the "Multiple Residence Law," which became effective on July 1, 1952, would be deemed to be a major capital improvement necessary for the preservation or maintenance of the housing accommodations and resulting in an increase in the rental value of the housing accommodations within the meaning of Section 33(1) of the Regulations. Since the improvement is required by law for the protection of the tenants, the consent of the tenants is not required. 

If the landlord requests information dealing with this opinion, he should be advised that upon furnishing details of the alterations, plans and costs, he may obtain a prior opinion which will indicate the rent increases which would be allowed upon completion of the improvement. Upon receipt of such a written prior opinion and after the work has been completed, he may then file an application with the Local Rent Office for the increases contained in the prior opinion.

Generally, the increase in the maximum rents to be granted by the Local Rent Administrator will permit the landlord to recover the cost of the improvement over a period of not more than ten years and will be apportioned equitably among all units in the building, including commercial and professional units. 300

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

er Further information and any necessary forms in accordance with this Opinion may be secured at the specific 

Local Rent Office having jurisdiction over the area in which the building is located. 

§ 33(1)-Increased Services or Equipment as Consideration for Execu tion of Voluntary Leases Not a Basis for Adjustment of Maximum 

Rents Under Section 33(1) of the Regulations. Administrator's Opinion No. 92. 

The maximum rent for an apartment was $40.00 per month. On March 15, 1951 the landlord and tenant entered into a lease which complied with all of the requirements of 

Section 33(2) of the Regulations. As a consideration for the execution of the lease the landlord replaced the mechanical refrigerator and gave the tenant the privilege of installing and maintaining a television antenna on the roof of the landlord's premises. These terms were contained in the written lease which provided for a monthly rent of $46.00. Since the basic March 1, 1943 maximum rent was increased 15% by reason of the voluntary lease, the Equalization Adjustment provision of the Act does not result in a change of the maximum rent. The landlord requests advice as to whether he may obtain an adjustment of the maximum rent after May 1, 1953 pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Regulations for the replacement of the mechanical refrigerator and the privilege of maintaining the television antenna on the roof of his premises. 

The landlord may not obtain any increase in the maximum rent. When the landlord entered into the voluntary lease, the additional equipment and services were merged into the overall rent provided by the lease. The additional equipment and services have become incorporated in the rent established by the execution of the lease which resulted in the landlord obtaining a higher maximum rent. 301

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

§ 33(1) Installation of Air-Conditioning Unit by Tenant as Basis for Increase of Maximum Rent. Administrator's Opinion No. 95. (Revised) 

(1) A tenant who pays for the electricity supplied to his apartment directly to the utility company wishes to install an air-conditioning unit which will rest on part of the window sill. The unit will not be entirely within the window line but will protrude beyond the window sill, or if it does not protrude, it will be attached to the outside of the building, rather than being secured from the inside. Will an order increasing the maximum rent pursuant to Section 

33(1) of the Regulations be issued by the Local Rent Administrator on the landlord's application by reason of the installation of the air-conditioning unit by the tenant in the manner described?

Yes. The air-conditioning unit, if installed in the manner described by the tenant, will not be incidental to the tenant's enjoyment of his housing accommodation since the air-conditioner as installed by the tenant will protrude beyond the confines of the space rented to the tenant under his rental agreement with his landlord. Where a landlord has consented to the utilization of such additional facility by the tenant, he may properly apply for a $2.00 per month rent adjustment pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Regula tions on the basis of such increase in services, etc.

Prior to this revision of Administrator's Opinion No. 95 

the Commission had no uniform rule with respect to the matter of increasing maximum rents due to tenant-installation of air-conditioning units. This lack of uniformity resulted from apparently conflicting court decisions. The 

Appellate Division, First Department, in Matter of Victory 

Hldg. Corporation (Herman), New York Law Journal, June 

14, 1961, p. 13, c. 5 and 6, stated that: 

Filing instructions: Insert this Rent Administrator", Third Edition, material immediately between pages December 1, 1957. 

42 and 43 of "Opinions of the State 302

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

"*** Both cases involved squatter proceedings to determine whether the tenants, by installing air conditioners, unlawfully trespassed upon realty remaining in the landlord's possession. Neither decision purported to determine whether 'the tenant has accepted and is obtaining the benefit of increased services, furniture, furnishings or equipment' within the meaning of section 33(1)(a) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations. All they decided was whether the landlord had to right to withhold consent to installation, not whether it could charge an additional rental for consent. Consequently, the decisions do not compel or authorize the rent administrator discriminatorily to allow rent increases in the boroughs included in the Second Department for any installation extending beyond the building line and to deny such increases in Manhattan and the Bronx unless the installation extends more than six inches beyond the window sill. The rent administrator could not reasonably apply two different rules without being responsible for illegal discrimination, ***”. Accordingly, the Administrator has adopted the following uniform rule concerning the eligibility of landlords to receive rent adjustments pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Regulations on the basis of tenant-installation of air-conditioning units:

Where a tenant has installed an air-conditioning unit which protrudes beyond the window line, or is attached to the exterior of the building even though there is no protrusion, the Local Rent Administrator will on application of the landlord order a rent increase of $2.00 per month, which increase shall remain effective so long as the tenant remains in occupancy. For the purpose of this opinion the term "window line" shall mean the outside facing of the building wall unless the window sill protrudes beyond 303

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

the building wall. In the latter case the "window line" shall mean the outer edge of the window sill. (2) Where the landlord furnishes unmetered and unlimited electric service to the tenant under a rent inclusion plan, the Local Rent Administrator will approve increases for the electricity to be consumed in the operation of the air-conditioning unit in the following amounts: 

1/3 H.P. Air-conditioning Unit for the months of May through $3.50 per month 

September, inclusive 

1/2 H.P. Air-conditioning Unit 

4.50 per month for the months of May through 

September, inclusive 

3/4 H.P. Air-conditioning Unit 

7.00 per month for the months of May through 

September, inclusive 

1 H.P. Air-conditioning Unit 

9.00 per month for the months of May through 

September, inclusive

The foregoing allowances have been calculated on the basis of average normal use of a window-type room air conditioner in New York City during the period from May through September.

The order which will be issued by the Local Rent Administrator will include an additional allowance for the privilege of maintaining the air-conditioning unit in the window where such allowance is proper and an allowance for the electric current which will be consumed by the operation of the unit in accordance with the foregoing schedule. 

The total annual increase may be allocated on an equal monthly basis by the Local Rent Administrator where the landlord and tenant request the adjustment of the maximum 304

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

rent on that basis prior to the issuance of the order increasing the maximum rent.

All orders granting increases of the maximum rent on the basis of the installation by the tenant of air-conditioning units will remain in effect only so long as the tenant referred to in the order remains in occupancy. Where such tenant removes from the apartment and a new tenant takes possession the maximum rent will be reduced automatically by the amount of adjustment provided for in such order. 

In the event that the new tenant should also install an airconditioning unit the maximum rent will automatically be again increased by the amount of the previous adjustment therefor from the date of such installation. (Issued June 15, 1961.) 

§ 33(1)-Partial Commercial Use—Adjustments of Maximum Rent. Administrator's Opinion No. 100. 

A landlord has requested advice as to whether premises which are used by a tenant for combined business and dwelling purposes, and are subject to the Regulations, may have their maximum rents increased because of such usage. 

In general, this is dependent upon the situation in existence on March 1, 1950, the date determining the maximum rent. If on and prior to that date an apartment was actually used for combination residential and professional or commercial purposes the landlord may not obtain any increase in the maximum rent since this usage is a service actually enjoyed by the tenant on that date. 

However, where on March 1, 1950, the apartment was used solely for residential purposes and the tenant subsequently commences to use the housing accommodations for mixed residential and professional or commercial purposes and does derive some income from this usage, the landlord is entitled to an adjustment pursuant to Section 33(1). 

—20 

305

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

The Local Rent Administrator should inquire into the extent of the additional usage of the apartment and of the common facilities of the building by members of the public who deal with the tenant in connection with the practice of his profession or business. Where this is completely lacking, as for example in the case of a professional writer who does his writing in his home, no adjustment should be granted. Inquiry should also be made into whether the tenant has an office elsewhere in which he practices his profession or business, and whether he advertises his home as his office or place of business. For example, if the tenant is an architect who uses his home as his office, and there is the element of income derived by him of using his home as his office an adjustment is proper. 

On the other hand, if the tenant teaches drafting and prepares his work assignments in his home, no adjustment would be proper.

The Local Rent Administrator will consider all the equities in determining the amount of the adjustment. In order to establish uniform administration it is suggested that a 10% increase would be proper where the tenant derives only part of his income from his profession or the conduct of his business in the apartment. The first type of case would be that in which a dentist who had an office elsewhere had also developed the custom of seeing an occasional patient in the evening at his home, and has therefore installed a dental chair, etc. This would be a limited usage which would warrant a 10% increase. 

On the other hand, if the only office of the dentist were in his own home, and the dentist were not in the category of a retired dentist who occasionally does a little work, it would follow that a substantial income would be derived by him from the practice of his profession, and that therefore an adjustment of 33 1/3% of the maximum rent would be equitable. 306

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

§ 33(1)—Rewiring of an Inadequately Wired Building by Landlord as Basis for Increase of Maximum Rent Under Section 33(1)(c) of the Regulations. Administrator's Opinion No. 117 (Revised). 

A landlord requests advice from a Local Rent Office as to whether the rewiring of an inadequately wired building would constitute a major capital improvement within the contemplation of Section 33(1)(c) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations for which the consent of the tenants of the building would not be required. He also asks the amount of rent increase which would be granted for such rewiring. 

The Administrator has consistently held that the rewiring of an inadequately wired building is a major capital improvement reasonably required for the preservation and maintenance of a building and that it is not necessary for the landlord to obtain the consent of his tenants for such improvement and the resulting increase in rent. This position was adopted because the tremendous and continuously accelerating increase in tenants' use of electric current is rendering rewiring necessary for the operation, preservation and maintenance of many structures. Wiring systems installed relatively recently, as well as earlier installations, have become inadequate to supply needed electricity. Since inadequate wiring reduces voltage, appliances frequently give unsatisfactory performance and are often damaged. 

Far more serious, however, are the fire hazards which exist in inadequately wired buildings. These hazards increase as tenants resort to dangerous expedients in order to plug in more attachments and appliances than existing wiring can safely accommodate.

The Commission has had over five years of operating experience with this complex problem, and after extensive and independent research, is now making changes in rewiring policies and procedures.

A variety of rewiring programs have been considered by 307

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

the Commission during this five year period and the Administrator has determined as a result of his study of the problem that the rewiring of an inadequately wired building should make provision for immediate and reasonably foreseeable anticipated needs of the tenants for an adequate supply of electric current. The rewiring of a building will therefore only be deemed to be a major capital improvement within the meaning of Section 33(1) (c) of the Regulations where the following minimum installations are provided for: (a) three #8 sub-feeder (riser) wires (or wiring of equivalent electrical capacity) to supply current to the apartment panel box; (b) one heavy duty appliance circuit with one or two duplex outlets in each kitchen, located at a place which is readily accessible to a table or work area and not installed over a sink or stove or behind a refrigerator; and (c) Where there has been only one branch circuit for all existing outlets in an apartment such circuit shall be split so as to provide two branch circuits in addition to the new circuit and outlets installed in the kitchen, unless the new circuit to the kitchen consists of one 

3-wire, or two 2-wire appliance branch circuits.

Initially in calculating the rental value for such rewiring a cost amortization basis had been adopted which provided for a seven year recoupment of the investment. After detailed study and investigation, it was determined in 1957 that a rental value schedule should be established governing rent adjustments for rewiring as the most effective method of eliminating abuses which had seriously impaired the merit of the cost recoupment formula. Under the earlier arrangement, the costs of electric rewiring had tended to become excessive and continued to rise. The limited number 308

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

of electrical contractors doing this work resulted in the absence of true competition in this branch of the industry. 

Moreover, it was found that the landlord lacked his usual incentive to keep his costs to the absolute minimum since rent increases were based upon the amount of his expenditure. Hidden finance charges and other collusive agreements resulted in exaggerated costs.

Ample precedent existed for the establishment of a rental value for electrical rewiring since many items of service and equipment have specific amounts established in the Rental Value Schedule of the Commission. In 1957 it was determined that $2.75 per month per dwelling unit fairly represented the added rental value to an apartment which had been properly rewired to meet the needs of modern appliances, provided that the rewiring met the standards of the electrical code. That determination was made after a full year of investigation and analysis of various sources of information including records of hundreds of applications processed by the Commission.

Notwithstanding the establishment of this fixed rental value for rewiring the Commission continued with its study of the problem for the purpose of uncovering any inequities which might have resulted from the departure from its previous seven year recoupment formula. After a further study based upon an additional two years operating experience and information supplied by the industry with respect to cost factors the Administrator now finds that the cost of installation of the rewiring bears a direct relation to the size and height of the structure. From the foregoing the Administrator concludes that the $2.75 allowance which had been adopted did not offer the economic incentive to owners of certain structures to meet the needs of their tenants for additional electric current. Therefore, the Commission's rewiring policies and procedures are being mod- 309

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

ified not only with respect to the minimum installation which shall be deemed to be a major capital improvement; but, also with respect to the rent increases which may be granted for such rewiring.

The Administrator has accordingly determined that the following Schedule of Rental Values shall be applicable to electrical rewiring: 

MONTHLY 

$2.75* 3.00* $4.00* (a) for buildings containing less than 65 dwelling units and less than 8 stories in height (b) for buildings containing 65 or more dwelling units and less than 8 stories in height (c) for buildings of 8 or more stories in height..

In those instances where a landlord does not wish to undertake such a major capital improvement under the allowances set forth in the above rental schedule, he has access to 

Section 33(1)(d) of the Regulations. This section provides that where a landlord secures the written consent of 75% of the tenants he may apply for the agreed rent increases; in such instances, the rental schedule herein will not apply. 

The rental schedule herein will be applicable to all buildings except where it is found that because of unique and peculiar combination of factors, such as a building with a majority of the apartments containing over six rooms, many with a substantial professional or commercial use, the foregoing allowances may not be appropriate and such facts had been submitted to the Administrator for a prior opinion before the commencement of the rewiring. $1.00 additional each month for each air conditioning circuit and outlet installed by the landlord at the tenant's request. For this additional allowance the landlord will be obligated to install all needed wiring within the apartment from the fuse panel box to the tenant's air conditioning unit or place where the tenant proposes to install the air conditioner. 310

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

The rewiring contemplated under this Opinion is expected to be of a capacity at the apartment distribution panel sufficient to permit the installation of an air conditioning circuit in any apartment in the building. Such rewiring implies the consent of the landlord to the installation of an air conditioning unit by the tenant. When the building is being rewired the landlord must comply with the tenant's request to install an air conditioning circuit and outlet. If such request is made at any time after the building is rewired and the landlord does not elect to make such installation, the tenant must be permitted to have such installation made by a licensed electrician at his own expense and without being obligated to pay any additional increase to the landlord for the installation of the air conditioning circuit and outlet. Where the landlord installs the necessary wiring and outlet within the apartment for the purpose of connecting the tenant's air conditioning unit with the fuse panel box, an additional allowance of $1.00 above the rent increase for the major capital improvement will be granted after completion of such work even though the tenant has not actually made the installation of the air conditioning unit. However, in no case shall a tenant be compelled to pay an additional increase of $1.00 a month for the installation of the air conditioning circuit and outlet in the absence of his written consent to such installation. It should be noted, that the landlord may also be entitled to further rent increases based upon the tenant's installation of an air conditioning unit as set forth in Administrator's Opinion No. 95.

PROCEDURES 

It will not be necessary for a landlord to request a prior opinion in rewiring applications where the rental schedule 311

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

herein is applicable. After completion of the rewiring, a landlord may file his application for rental increases pursuant to Section 33(1)(c) of the Regulations, together with a list of tenants, identified by apartment designation, who have consented in writing to pay an additional rent increase for the installation of an air conditioning circuit and outlet. 

In his application he will be required to describe briefly the type of wiring which had previously been in the building and the type of heavier wiring now installed. This description must include a specific statement as to the capacity formerly available and the capacity available as a result of the rewiring and proof that the rewiring system meets the minimum requirements of the local electrical code and of this Opinion. The landlord must establish the substantial improvement to the kitchen and apartment wiring before he becomes entitled to the increase set forth in this Opinion. 

Where the tenant, with the permission of the landlord, has already installed a proper circuit and outlet for air conditioning at his own expense, he will not be required to pay any additional charge for the air conditioning circuit and outlet which may be installed as a replacement.

The Administrator deems it proper to note that a landlord who has rewired a building and desires to receive rent adjustments pursuant to this Opinion, cannot unreasonably deny any tenant in the building the right to install air conditioning. The landlord may not demand, as a condition of his permission, that the tenant sign a lease increasing the maximum rent pursuant to Section 33 (2) of the Regulations.

It is also proper to note that as a condition of receiving orders increasing maximum rents the landlord must certify and establish that he is maintaining essential services.

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS § 33(1)-Effect of Tax Abatement on Rent Increases Otherwise Allowable for the Installation of Central Heating and Hot Water Systems and Other Improvements. Administrator's Opinion No. 120 (For 

New York City only).

The Facts: 

A ten-family house in the City of New York has had a central heating and hot water system installed recently in contemplation of the requirements of the New York City Multiple Dwelling Code. The landlord has filed an application for rent increases pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations. The landlord states that he will apply to the City of New York for tax abatement relief (See Local Law No. 688 for the Year 1956; § J41-2.4 of the New York City Administrative Code). The Local 

Rent Administrator requests advice as to the amount of rent increases he may allow by reason of the installation of the central heating and hot water system or for any other improvement to a property where the landlord receives tax abatement.

Opinion: 

The problem presented here is one which is peculiar to the City of New York. It arises from the requirement of the New York City Multiple Dwelling Code that multiple dwellings containing ten or more units have central heating and hot water systems installed by November 1, 1958 and buildings containing less than ten units have such installations by November 1, 1959. As a corollary to this law the 

City also made provision for a tax exemption for improvements (defined in the law to be physical changes in an existing dwelling other than painting, ordinary repairs, normal replacement or maintenance items) for a twelve-year period and for further relief by way of tax abatement which will permit the recovery of 75% of the cost of the improvement 313

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

over a period of nine years. This tax abatement provision applies not only to central heating and hot water but to other improvements such as the installation of of private toilets or any other improvement for which the City will allow relief. However, in order to obtain this relief the landlord is required by the Administrative Code (§ J41-2.4) 

to do the following: (1) obtain a certificate from the City Planning Commission that the improvement "will not unduly interfere with projected public improvements or the clearance and rebuilding of substandard and insanitary areas"; (2) obtain from the Department of Buildings a certificate that the building is structurally sound, complies with applicable provisions of law and provides central or other appropriate and approved heating; (3) obtain a certification from the Department of Buildings of the reasonable cost of the improvement; and (4) file an application with the Tax Commission between 

February 1st and March 15th for the relief afforded by

SJ41-2.4 of the Administrative Code. 

In a report prepared by the City Administrator in connection with the legislation referred to above, entitled "Leg islative Program To Improve Housing In New York City," 

it was stated that: "This legislation will facilitate financing for owners of cold-water buildings who will be required by the new Multiple Dwelling Code to install central heating and hot water plants. It will also help owners who desire to rehabilitate and improve substandard multiple dwellings. 

The purpose of the improvements must be to remove fire or health hazards, or to replace inadequate or obsolete sanitary facilities.” It was further stated in the report that: "By enabling owners to amortize 75% of the cost of im- 314

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

provements through tax reductions, two important objectives are achieved: First, the cost of the improvements to the owner is diminished by three-quarters; second, rent increases to tenants in these properties will be substantially lower than they would otherwise be, since the net cost of the improvements to the owner is a small fraction of the full amount."

In establishing existing rental increases incident to improvements of the type affected by tax abatement, the Administrator has been mindful of the fact that many of the housing accommodations involved afford shelter to lowincome tenants. Accordingly, rental increases have been carefully scrutinized by the Administrator and set at the lowest level consistent with giving due consideration to the landlord's recoupment of capital costs and costs of operation and maintenance, if any. Since a portion of the cost of the capital improvement will be reimbursed to the landlord through tax abatement, it is clearly equitable to reduce the rental increase normally allowed for the improvement.

The Administrator, therefore, in giving effect to the legislative admonition to prevent undue "hardship and dislocation" in the administration of the Emergency Housing Rent Control Law and bearing in mind the intent of the City administration in enacting the tax abatement legislation, has determined that the tenants should receive the benefit of two-thirds of the tax abatement allowed to the landlord for the improvement. This will accomplish two things. As a result of this formula the increase in rents for low-income tenants resulting from such improvements will be less. 

Meanwhile landlords who avail themselves of tax abatement will recoup their investment over a shorter period of time because of the combination of the permitted rent increases plus tax abatement. 315

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

In a proper case the City will allow the landlord a tax abatement of 75% of the reasonable cost of the improvement amortized over a period of nine years. That means that for every $1,000.00 found to be the reasonable cost of the improvement the City will yield the landlord $750.00 in tax abatement via annual tax remissions of $83.33 each year. 

The Commission will, however, allow two-thirds of this tax abatement to be used to offset any rent increase which would otherwise be allowable for the improvement. Concretely, assume at situation were presented to the Local Rent Administrator where a building consisting of ten fiveroom apartments qualifies for tax abatement and the landlord was granted such abatement in the sum of $360.00 for each year. This translated into monthly rents for each of the ten apartments would indicate that the monthly tax abatement allocable to each apartment would be $3.00. Twothirds of this amount, or $2.00 per month, would be deducted from the amount the tenant would be required to pay in the absence of tax abatement.

Where tax abatement has been granted by the City and the landlord is already receiving the benefits thereof the rent increase orders will reflect a rent increase which is modified by tax abatement. Where tax abatement has been granted by the City which will become effective on the following July first the rent increase orders will reflect tax abatement effective on the date when the tax abatement goes into effect.

Where tax abatement has been allowed for an improvement which was the basis for a previous rent increase, the Local Rent Administrator will re-open the proceeding in order to give the tenant credit for two-thirds of the tax abatement allowed pursuant to the second unnumbered paragraph of Section 33 of the Rent and Eviction Regulations which reads as follows: 316

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

"The Administrator shall have the power to revoke or modify any adjustment granted hereunder if there has been substantial change in the basis upon which such adjustment was granted." 

In computing this credit the Local Rent Administrator will base his computation upon the annual tax abatement allowed by the City. This amount will be allocated to each tenant on a monthly basis. This monthly credit will be used to reduce the rental increase previously ordered and the maximum rent will be accordingly modified effective with the date of the order unless the tax year in which tax abatement has been granted has not already commenced. In the latter case the modification will take effect on July first following the date of issuance of the order.

Issued December 19, 1957. 

§ 33(1)—Replacement of Plumbing as Basis for Increase of Maximum Rent Under Section 33(1)(c) of the Regulations. Administrator's Opinion No. 121. 

A landlord requests advice from a Local Rent Office whether the replacement of existing plumbing consisting of galvanized iron piping by a new brass plumbing system would be considered a major capital improvement within the contemplation of Section 33(1)(c) of the Rent and Eviction 

Regulations. He further inquires as to how much he could expect by way of rent increases if such plumbing improvement is considered to be a major capital improvement. 

Faulty plumbing accelerates the deterioration of buildings because of the frequency of leaks and even though repairs are promptly made, water seeps through floors, plaster and walls. The dampness thus created causes rotting of the exposed parts of the building and is conducive to infestation. 

Galvanized iron piping being susceptible to corrosion, the 317

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

installation of long lasting, rust resisting, brass or copper piping has become an accepted practice in modern building construction. Therefore, the Administrator finds that the installation of new brass or copper piping in place of existing galvanized iron piping is required for the operation, preservation or maintenance of buildings and accordingly is a major capital improvement within the meaning of Section 33(1)(c) of the Regulations where made as a part of a comprehensive plan to preserve the structure. To qualify as a major capital improvement plumbing installation must be a part of a coordinated building wide, consecutively timed, project. Piecemeal repairs will not so qualify. This determination conforms with the Commission's general policy of encouraging landlords to take affirmative steps to preserve and improve the existing housing supply. 

The Administrator has recognized the following plumbing installations to be major capital improvements. He has also given consideration to the current costs for making such improvements and has determined that for those commenced on or after January 1, 1959 the following Rental 

Values shall be applicable: 318

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS NEW BRASS OR COPPER PLUMBING IMPROVEMENTS 

Basement Piping without new Water Service installation Basement Piping with new Water Service installation 

Risers (concealed) 

(exposed replacing exposed)

Branches to Fixtures (concealed) MONTHLY 

$ .75 1.00 2.00 1.00 2.25 .75 (exposed replacing exposed)

Trim in Kitchen and one Bathroom [includes modern faucets for all sinks and tubs together with modernization of shower (new head, body, arm and concealed brass supply)] .75 

The above rental values include costs for the proper restoration of floors, walls and ceilings incidental to such improvements.

The rental schedule herein will apply to all buildings except where it is found that because of a unique and peculiar combination of factors (such as a building with a majority of the apartments containing more than one bathroom or a toilet in addition to a bathroom) the foregoing allowances may not be appropriate and such facts are submitted to the Administrator for a prior opinion before the commencement of the plumbing improvement.

PROCEDURES 

It will not be necessary for a landlord to request a prior opinion in plumbing improvement applications where the rental schedule herein is applicable. After the completion of such improvement, a landlord should file his application for rental increases pursuant to Section 33(1)(c) of the Regulations which does not require the consent of the tenants. 319

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

In his application the landlord will briefly describe the type of plumbing which was previously supplied to the building and a description of the plumbing improvement which is the basis for the application. In addition the landlord will be required to file Form 33(1) Supplement in duplicate after completion by the plumbing contractor. 

The Administrator deems it proper to note that as a condition of receiving orders increasing maximum rents the landlord must certify and establish that he is maintaining essential services.

§ 33 (2)-Prior Opinions. Administrator's Opinion No. 37 [Revoked]. 

§ 33 (2)-Effect of Escalator Clauses in Previous Leases. 

tor's Opinion No. 73. 

Administra- 

A landlord requests advice as to whether the voluntary lease increase authorized under Section 33 (2) of the new Regulations permits the landlord to collect an automatic increase in rent pursuant to the following types of escalator clauses: 

1. The tenant is in possession under a lease, the term of which has not yet expired. This lease contains the following clause: "Notwithstanding the rent specified in this lease (which is the present maximum legal rent for the demised premises under existing law), tenant agrees to pay to the landlord any higher legally permissible rent or any higher maximum legal rent for the demised premises which it may become legally permissible to charge. Such increased rent shall be the rent due and payable hereunder as of the date on which it becomes legally permissible to charge said increased rent." 

2. The tenant is in possession under a lease, the term of which has not yet expired. This lease contains the following clause: "If, by operation of law, it becomes per- 320

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

missible to charge a higher rent than the present maximum legal rent for the demised premises conditioned upon the landlord and tenant entering into a new agreement of lease upon such conditions as to effective date, term or otherwise as may be specified by applicable law or regulations issued thereunder, tenant agrees at the option of landlord and upon demand, to enter into such a lease at the permissible increased maximum legal rent for the demised premises and thereupon said new lease shall supersede the present lease." 

3. The tenant is in possession under a lease, the term of which has not yet expired. This lease contains a clause which reads substantially as follows: "Notwithstanding the rent specified in this lease (which is the present maximum legal rent collectible under existing law), tenant agrees to pay to the landlord when it may become legally permissible to collect same, the monthly rent of $. . that being the maximum rent as established by order of The Office of The Housing Expediter. Such increased rent shall be the rent due and payable hereunder as of the date on which it becomes legally permissible to charge such increased rent." 

4. The tenant is in possession as a statutory tenant, since his lease has expired and has not been renewed. A clause similar to one of the three cited above appeared in the lease under which this statutory tenant previously held possession.

There is no automatic increase in rent authorized under the Regulations. A landlord may not demand or receive more than the present maximum rent in any of these cases. 

These escalator clauses cannot be deemed voluntary written agreements within the meaning of Section 33(2). This section of the Regulations contemplates a new voluntary lease executed by landlord and tenant in accordance with 

-21 

321

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

the provisions of that section of the Regulations. The escalator clauses referred to above are therefore inoperative to automatically increase the maximum rent. 

§ 33(2)-Cancellation Clauses in Voluntary Leases. 

Opinion No. 74. 

Administrator's 

A landlord requests advice as to whether certain types of clauses giving the landlord the right to cancel, may be inserted in voluntary leases executed under Section 33(2) 

of the Regulations. 

The first proposed clause reads as follows: 

"In the event that during the term hereof the rent herein fixed shall, for reasons other than the fault of the landlord, be in excess of the amount legally permitted to be collected for the leased premises, the landlord may upon determination that the rent is excessive, terminate this lease upon ten days' written notice and at the expiration of such ten days the lease shall terminate as though that were the date originally fixed herein for its termination and thereupon the tenant shall be a statutory tenant for such term and upon such conditions as the statute may provide."

The second proposed clause reads as follows: 

"If, by reason of any legislative enactment of the Federal, State or City Government or governmental regulation of any agency of the Federal, State or City Government, or otherwise, it shall become unlawful or impossible for the landlord to collect the full amount of the rent herein provided, the landlord shall have the right to cancel this lease on 10 days' notice in writing to the tenant."

The inclusion of either of the above clauses or any similar clauses would be in violation of the express provisions of 

Section 33(2) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations. 

Section 15 of the Regulations specifically provides that 

322

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

"An agreement by the tenant to waive the benefit of any provision of the Act or these Regulations is void." A tenant may not, therefore, agree to waive any provision of the Regulations except that he may agree to pay an increase in the maximum rent not to exceed 15 percent, provided he secures a lease for a period of at least two years upon the conditions specified in Section 33(2). 

§ 33 (2)-Escalator Clauses in Voluntary Leases in Contemplation of Future Increased Services or Equipment. Administrator's Opinion No. 76 [Revoked]. See Administrator's Opinion No. 76A Under this Section Where Lease Was Executed on or after April 1, 1953. 

§ 33(2)—Clause in Voluntary Lease in Contemplation of Future Increased Services or Equipment. Administrator's Opinion No. 76A (Revised). 

On February 1, 1959 a tenant entered into a voluntary lease agreement for the two-year term commencing February 1, 1959 and increasing the maximum rent from $100.00 per month to $115.00 per month. The lease also provides that: "32. The tenant further agrees to pay as additional rent such amount as may be fixed by order of the Temporary State Housing Rent Commission for increased services or equipment to which the tenant has consented pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Rent and Eviction Reg ulations."

About a year after the execution of the statutory lease, the landlord rewired the electrical circuit in the building without having obtained the tenant's written consent. The landlord made application pursuant to Section 33(1)(c) of the Regulations on the basis of his having made a major capital improvement required for the preservation of the building, namely the rewiring of the electrical circuit in the building, for which the tenants' consent is not required. On 

May 15, 1960 the Administrator issued an order adjusting the maximum rent from $115.00 to $117.75 per month pursu- 323

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

ant to Section 33(1)(c) of the Regulations based upon the electric circuit rewiring in the building. 

The landlord has served notice upon the tenant that commencing June 1, 1960 his monthly rent will be $117.75.

The tenant requests advice concerning his obligation to pay the higher rent demanded by his landlord. 

The Legislature in making provision for rent increases based upon mutual voluntary lease agreements between landlords and tenants [Section 4(4)(a)(5) of the Act] contemplated that a tenant who executes a statutory lease would have the contractual right to remain in possession of the leased accommodations at the rental agreed upon in the lease for the duration of the lease term which rent was not to exceed a 15 percent increase in the maximum rent. Thus, if the landlord were eligible for a further increase in the maximum rent during the lease period through some other adjustment provision of the Regulations, he would not be able to compel the tenant to pay the higher maximum rent during the lease term without the tenant's written consent. 

Also, it would not be proper for the lease to provide for a blanket consent via a so-called "escalator clause." [See A'd ministrator's Opinion No. 73.] Accordingly, the landlord may not compel the tenant to pay any additional rent on the basis of the rewiring of the electrical circuit until the expiration of the lease. 

It is to be noted, however, that a landlord and tenant may always modify or cancel a lease by a mutual voluntary agreement. This may be done either by a separate written agreement or by the tenant's endorsement of his written consent on the landlord's application for rent adjustment pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Regulations. Such a consent effects a valid modification of the lease and is deemed to be equivalent to an express agreement by the tenant to pay additional rent for the increased service or facilities, sub- 324

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

stantial rehabilitation, major capital or other improvements described in the landlord's application in an amount to be fixed by order of the Administrator. If the landlord's application for rent adjustment is under any other provision than Section 33(1) of the Regulations, the modification or cancellation of the lease should be accomplished by the execution of a separate written agreement. 

This is different from the case where the parties at the time of making the rental agreement make provision in the agreement for the payment of additional rent for either (1) a specified substantial increase in dwelling space or an increase in the services, furniture, furnishings or equipment provided in the housing accommodations at or prior to the execution of the lease to which the tenant has consented in writing or (2) a major capital improvement or a substantial rehabilitation of the building or housing accommodations therein which was either completed or in progress when the lease was executed. In these situations the landlord has, in effect, the right of having the maximum rent increased by order of the Administrator pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Regulations. In such cases there is a complete understanding between the landlord and tenant concerning the tenant's obligation to pay additional rent. The tenant has bargained to pay for certain specified changes in the housing accommodations. The tenant's liability for additional rent is measured by the rent adjustment to be ordered by the Administrator. 

§ 33(2)—Rooming Houses; Effect of Lease Increase for One Rate Upon Other Rates. Administrator's Opinion No. 77 (Revised). 

A room in a rooming house is registered at $15.00 per week for one-person occupancy; $17.00 per week for twoperson occupancy; and $18.00 per week for three-person occupancy. The present tenant who occupies the room at 325

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

the two-person rate has executed a lease with the landlord which complies with all of the requirements of Section 33(2) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations and provides for a weekly rental of $19.55 (15% above the $17.00 per week rate for two-person occupancy). The landlord now requests advice as to whether the execution of the least operates to increase the other two rates by 15% so that in the event that the tenant vacates, the landlord upon re-renting at a threeperson rate may automatically collect 15% above $18.00 or $20.70 per week.

The execution of the statutory lease ordinarily would operate to increase the maximum rent only for the two-person rate in effect immediately prior to the execution of the lease. However, since the new maximum rent for the twoperson occupancy would be higher than the three-person maximum rent in effect immediately prior to the execution of the lease it would be inequitable to prohibit a landlord from collecting the higher two-person maximum rent even though the housing accommodations were subsequently rented for a three-person occupancy. The Administrator is therefore of the opinion that the execution of the statutory lease also has the effect of automatically increasing the three-person weekly rate to $19.55 so that the weekly maximum rent for both two-person occupancy and three-person occupancy would be the same. If the weekly rate for threeperson occupancy had been higher than $19.55, the execution of the lease would not have changed the three-person occupancy rate.

In connection with the foregoing it is important to note that the records of the Commission are automatically modified to conform with this Opinion. Thus, for example, where a room in a rooming house is registered at $15.00 per week for one-person occupancy; $19.55 per week for two-person 326

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

occupancy; and $18.00 per week for three-person occupancy, the registration statement will be modified without further proceedings by the Administrator to read $15.00 per week for one-person occupancy; $19.55 per week for two-person occupancy; and $19.55 per week for three-person occupancy. 

It is also to be noted that since the weekly one-person occupancy rate is less than the weekly two-person occupancy rate, the execution of the statutory lease does not change that rate. If the tenant vacates and the room is subsequently rented by the week for a one-person occupancy, that rate may be increased by a lease only where a new lease is executed which conforms with all the requirements of 

Section 33(2) of the Regulations. 

In situations where a room in a rooming house has daily and/or monthly rates as well as weekly rates for the same number of occupants the execution of a statutory lease would have the effect of increasing all rates for the same number of occupants by the same percentage. In which case the above formula would also apply to the daily and/or monthly rates and the records of the Commission would also be automatically modified with respect to the daily and/or monthly rates. In the following illustration line A represents the maximum rents in effect immediately prior to the execution of a state statutory lease increasing a twoperson weekly rate of $17.00 by 15%. Line B indicates the new maximum rents resulting from the execution of the lease. 

AB 2 Persons 3 Persons DAILY 

1 Person $2.50 $2.50 $2.75 $3.16 $3.00 $3.16 327

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS WEEKLY 1 Person 2 Persons 3 Persons 

AB AB $15.00 $17.00 $15.00 $19.55 $18.00 $19.55

§ 33(2)—Removal of Television Antenna by Landlord Where Installed Prior to March 1, 1950. See Administrator's Opinion No. 75 Under Section 24. 

$33(2)-Effect of Execution of Subsequent Lease Prior to the Expiration of the First Two Years of Prior Lease. Administrator's Opin ion No. 93. 

On June 1, 1951 a tenant entered into a voluntary 15% lease which complied with all of the requirements of Section 33(2) of the Regulations. The rent provided for in the lease was $46.00 per month and there has been no change in the maximum rent since the effective date of said lease. The tenant vacated the apartment on April 1, 1953 and it is now vacant. The landlord now desires to rent the housing accommodations to a new tenant and proposes to enter into a new voluntary lease for a term commencing April 15, 1953 and expiring May 30, 1955 at a monthly rental of $46.00 until June 1, 1953 and of $52.90, which is a further increase of 15%, from June 1, 1953 to the expiration of the lease. He requests advice as to whether the new voluntary lease will be effective to increase the maximum rent to $52.90 on June 1, 1953.

Yes. The execution of a lease as indicated will have the 328

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

effect of increasing the maximum rent pursuant to Section 

33(2) of the Regulations to $52.90 on June 1, 1953. 

§ 33(2)—Lease Executed by Tenant Under Threat of Eviction Is Ineffectual to Increase Maximum Rents. Administrator's Opinion No. 109. 

A landlord of an apartment house in which a resident superintendent is required by law files an application for an Eviction Certificate pursuant to Section 54 of the Reg ulations, in which he claims he needs the apartment for a superintendent. Previously, the landlord had demanded that this particular tenant execute a 15% lease increase and the tenant had refused. After filing the application, the landlord advised the tenant that he would withdraw it if the tenant would now give him an increase by lease. 

Rather than be evicted, the tenant complied. The landlord thereafter filed an application against another tenant under Section 54. The first tenant now asks whether he must pay this additional 15% called for in the lease. 

Section 33(2) of the Regulations specifies that a lease agreement must be voluntary and in good faith. On these facts, it is clear that the tenant executed the lease under duress since there was an actual threat of eviction. The act of the tenant cannot be considered voluntary and the lease does not comply with the statutory requirements. 

It is, therefore, the opinion of the Administrator that the lease was ineffectual to increase the maximum rent for the apartment. The Commission may institute an administrative proceeding pursuant to Section 36 of the Regulations to determine the facts. Upon this statement of facts, the Commission would issue an order re-establishing the maximum rent at the original sum prior to the execution of the lease. 329

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS § 33(2)—Effect of the Execution of a Lease During the Pendency of Proceedings to Increase Rents Pursuant to Section 33(5); Meaning of 

Term "increase over the maximum rent then in effect.

" Administra tor's Opinion No. 119. 

On July 1, 1957 a landlord of an apartment house filed an application for rent increases pursuant to Section 33(5) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations. The tenant of an apartment has informed the landlord that he will vacate his apartment on November 30, 1957. The present maximum rent is $135.00 per month and the landlord anticipates an increase in rent to $155.25, effective September 1, 1957, pursuant to Section 33(5) of the Regulations. No order has as yet been issued and the landlord desires to re-rent the apartment to a new tenant without waiting for the orders granting the anticipated rent increase. Any orders increasing the maximum rents will be made effective as of September 1, 1957. The landlord seeks advice as to whether he can obtain a statutory lease increase of 15% from the new tenant based upon the anticipated increase in the maximum rent so as to be able to obtain a double 

15% increase. He submits two alternatives, as follows: "1. A lease for $135.00 plus the percentage of increase estimated by the landlord plus 15% with a savings clause prescribing the fact that the parties agree there is now an application under Section 33 (5) which will adjust the maximum rent if granted in an amount retroactive to September 1, 1957; that it is the intention that the rent to be paid by the tenant to the landlord shall at all times be no more than 15% above the award to be made in the pending proceeding. "2. The alternative is to write a lease at $135.00 plus 

15% with an escalator clause limited to the precise amount which will be granted in the new pending Section 33(5) 330

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

proceeding issued retroactive to a date prior to execution and negotiation of the lease." The inclusion of either of the above alternatives would be in violation of the expressed provisions of Section 33(2) 

of the Rent and Eviction Regulations. Section 33(2) spe cifically provides that the maximum rent shall be automatically increased by the execution of "said lease, provided such lease (b) Provides for a rent increase not in excess of 15 percent over the maximum rent then in effect; (e) Does not provide for the payment by the tenant of any rent in excess of the amount therein provided, unless the maximum rent is thereafter increased by order of the Administrator to which the tenant has consented pursuant to paragraph 1 of this section 

The first alternative violates subparagraph (b) of Sec tion 33(2). The 15% lease increase may not be superimposed on the anticipated higher rental which was not yet in effect since the lease increase in rent must be predicated upon the maximum rent which was actually in existence when its was executed. Although the Local Rent Administrator may thereafter order an increase in the rent retroactive to a date prior to the date when the lease was executed it cannot be said that the new rent was in existence when the lease was executed. The effect of a retroactive increase in rent is solely to permit the collection of the higher rent from the date prescribed as the effective date of the rent increase. Factually the new rent did not come into existence until the date of the order fixing it. In a similar situation the Administrator took the same position and was upheld by the Court (Diemac Holding Co. v Weaver, Supreme Court, New York County, Tilzer, J., NYLJ, June 11, 1957, p 6, cols 7 and 8; affirmed without opinion, App Div, 1st Dept NYLJ, October 10, 1957, p 6, col 3). 331

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

The second alternative proposal violates subparagraph (e) of Section 32(2) quoted above which permits such a further increase in a lease rent only where it is based on an order for increases in service, equipment, etc. to which the tenant has consented under Section 33(1). Obviously the tenant has not consented to the increase in this case.

istrator's Opinion No. 25 [Revoked]. 

Admin- § 33(5)—Valuation to be Used for the Computation of 4 Percent Return.

§ 33(5)—Application for Increase Not Proper Where Additional Services or Equipment Were Supplied at Time Order Adjusting Maximum Rent Was Granted Pursuant to Section 33(5). Administrator's Opin ion No. 108 (Revised).

The landlord of an apartment house installed new and superior modern gas ranges and new and superior mechanical refrigerators in several apartments, with the consent of the tenants in occupancy. Although the landlord would, presumably, have been entitled to increases in the maximum rents of the apartments pursuant to Section 33(1) because of these improvements, he did not file any applications for rent increases based upon these installations. Instead, the landlord filed an application for adjustments of the maximum rents of all the apartments in the building pursuant to Section 33(5), and orders were issued granting him increases to bring his net income to the 6% required by the Statute and Regulations. Shortly after receiving these increases under Section 33(5), the landlord filed new applications under the provisions of Section 33(1) for further increases of the newly established maximum rents only as to those units where he had previously installed the new and better equipment. Will orders be issued further increasing the rents of these apartments pursuant to Section 33(1)? 

No. The new maximum rents established pursuant to 

Section 33(5) includes all essential services, furniture, fur- 332

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

1 nishings and equipment provided, or required to be provided, on the date of the issuance of the orders. It therefore follows that a landlord cannot obtain rent increases based upon a claim of additional services or equipment which were being provided on this date. 

The landlord could have elected to first file his applications pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Regulations and, in a proper case, receive rental increases for those apartments which had received the new and superior equipment. Had he done so, he could have subsequently filed applications pursuant to Section 33(5), but the additional rental income would have been reflected in his later applications. 

To permit the landlord to obtain additional rent increases upon grounds for adjustment already in existence prior to the filing of the application pursuant to Section 33(5) would be to permit circumvention and misuse of the Section. 

However, it is to be noted that a landlord who has failed to apply for and obtain rent increases based upon the tenant having installed a television antenna or an air conditioner would not be barred from filing an application pursuant to Section 33(1) even if these installations were made prior to the application under Section 33(5). These installations remain revocable licenses until such time as they become services included with the maximum rent by rent adjustment order or lease increase granted which includes the additional service. The Administrator recognizes that there may be various reasons which induce a landlord to permit the tenant these facilities just so long as they remain revocable at will by the landlord. 

Issued May 16, 1960. 333

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS § 33(5)-Effect of the Execution of a Lease During the Pendency of Proceedings to Increase Rents Pursuant to Section 33(5). See Administrator's Opinion No. 119 Under Section 33(2). 

Section 34. Grounds for decrease of maximum rent. 

§ 34(2)-Decrease in Service Prior to March 1, 1950. 

Opinion No. 27. 

Administrator's

In February, 1950 a landlord installed a separate electric meter for his tenant, even though prior thereto the landlord was obligated to, and did furnish electricity. On 

March 1, 1950, the tenant paid for his own electricity. May the tenant then file an application for a rent reduction based upon a decrease in service? 

Yes. Section 34(2) of the Regulations gives him the right to file an application for a rent reduction since there has been a decrease of a service required to be provided, even if not actually provided, on March 1, 1950. 

§ 34(2)-Decrease in Service of Painting and Decorating Upon Renting to New Tenant. Administrator's Opinion No. 105 (For New York 

City only). 

On November 1, 1954 a tenant rented a vacant apartment and upon the landlord's refusal to paint and decorate the apartment, the tenant painted it at his own expense. After taking possession the tenant filed an application with the 

Local Rent Administrator for a reduction of the maximum rent for the apartment pursuant to Section 34(2) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations based upon a claimed decrease in the service of painting and decorating. What action will be taken by the Administrator upon this application of the tenant?

Should the tenant apply within one year from the time of rental he is entitled to relief. A copy of the application will be forwarded to the landlord and he will be required to produce his books and records showing the painting practice of the apartment. If this apartment was due for 334

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

a painting at the time the tenant took possession, the landlord's failure to paint and decorate would be deemed a reduction in the services required to be provided by the landlord. Within the City of New York there is a presumption that apartments in multiple dwelling houses were customarily painted, prior to March 1, 1943, at periodic two-year intervals. The Appellate Courts have sustained this position of the Administrator in Kathy Realty Corp. v. McGoldrick (281 App Div 850, 119 NYS2d 255). and 

Bersam Holding Corp. v. McGoldrick (128 NYS2d 557).

This presumption is subject to rebuttal by the production of the landlord of books and records establishing a longer periodic period prior to that date. In any event, the burden is upon the landlord to establish that the apartment was not as yet due for a painting as of the time the new tenant took possession. Should the landlord fail to establish this, it would follow that there had been a reduction in the service of painting and decorating. The landlord would be given an opportunity to promptly refund the actual cost to the tenant of painting and decorating the apartment providing the cost is reasonable. Should the landlord fail to do this, the Local Rent Administrator would issue an order reducing the maximum rent because of the failure of the landlord to maintain the essential service of painting and decorating. The landlord cannot relieve himself of this obligation to paint and decorate by inserting a clause in the lease whereby the tenant agrees to do the painting. 

§ 34(3)-Decrease of Maximum Rent for Illegal Rooming House Where 

Maximum Rents for Single-Room Occupancy Had Previously Been 

Registered or Established by Order. Administrator's Opinion No. 

112 (For New York City only).

In 1948 the owner of a one-family house rented the individual rooms in the house for single-room occupancy. 335

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

Ac- Under the Federal Rent Regulations this structure was considered to be a rooming house when so rented. cordingly, the owner registered the rents for the individual rooms with the Federal authorities, thereby establishing maximum rents for the rooms. Recently the Department of Housing and Buildings of the City of New York certified to the Commission that the single-room occupancy is a violation of local law. What action will be taken by the Commission?

The Administrator will institute action pursuant to Section 34(3) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations to decrease the maximum rents. That section authorizes the Administrator to order a decrease of the maximum rent on the ground that the housing accommodations are occupied in violation of law. It was not the intent of the Legislature to permit landlords to reap profits from the continued illegal operation of a building. Therefore, the maximum rents will be reduced to a level which would reflect the seriousness of the condition which is certified by the municipal department.

Section 34(3) provides that the Administrator may order a reduction in the maximum rent where "a municipal department having jurisdiction certifies that the housing accommodation is a fire hazard or is in continued dangerous condition or detrimental to life or health, or is occupied in violation of law." The same approach will be taken where any illegal condition is certified to the Administrator by the appropriate municipal department. 336

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

Section 35. Decrease of services; application, order or report. 

§ 35(1)—Change from Manually-Operated Elevators to Automatic Elevators. Administrator's Opinion No. 40. 

A landlord requests advice from a Local Rent Office as to whether he may convert from manually-operated elevators to automatic elevators. What shall we advise him?

No conversion from manually-operated elevators to automatic elevators may be made except by an order of the 

Local Rent Administrator. Section 24 of the Regulations requires that a landlord shall continue to provide with the housing accommodations the same essential services as were provided on the date determining the maximum rent. 

A change from a manually-operated elevator to an automatic elevator, depending upon the facts in the case, may constitute a decrease in service. It must therefore be undertaken only by an order pursuant to the requirements of Section 35(1) of the Regulations. The landlord should file an application requesting that an order be issued by the 

Local Rent Administrator permitting the change. The landlord may use a master application, but must furnish to the Local Rent Office sufficient copies so that one may be sent to each tenant. After the record has been thoroughly examined, the Local Rent Administrator may, in his discretion, either grant the application or deny it. 

The policy determination in the conversion from manually-operated to automatic elevators is whether it constitutes a decrease in services. The general character of the house, the number of elevators, the number of entrances and lobbies, and the other services performed by the elevator personnel are factors to be considered. There may not be a case of a decrease in services where upon conversion the overall service and the type of automatic elevators to be 

—22 

337

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

installed give the tenants substantially as good service as they previously enjoyed. However, the elimination of some or all of the elevator employees may result in a loss to the tenants of certain protection furnished by their presence and of various other services performed by them. 

An order permitting conversion would usually require that the building entrance or entrances and the lobby not be left unattended for any portion of the day or night. 

Such order may also require that the landlord install facilities such as inter-communicating system; vestibule, bell, and buzzer system; and U. S. Government-approved mail boxes; or other provisions for furnishing services previously performed by elevator employees. The establishment of these conditions in the order and the landlord's compliance therewith may constitute no decrease in services because of the conversion. 

Should an order be granted by the Local Rent Administrator, it shall clearly set forth the terms and conditions upon which the permission to convert is granted. In addition, the order should contain a statement that "This order does not affect any rights the tenant may have under any rental or other contractual agreement with the landlord." 

§ 35(1)—Change from Submetering Electrical Current to a Rent Inclusion Plan. Administrator's Opinion No. 80. 

A landlord requests advice from a Local Rent Office as to the procedure which he may follow due to the fact that a recent order of the Public Service Commission has the effect of prohibiting the submetering of electric current. 

He states that on the date determining the maximum rent he submetered the electrical current and has continued to charge for such service on the basis of the tenants' actual consumption; and that he desires to effectuate a change- 

. 

338

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

i over in the furnishing of electric service and adopt a rent inclusion plan with the tenants. What shall we advise him?

Under the Regulations, the housing accommodation has a varying rent since the varying charge for electrical current each month is added to the base rent of the apartment which thereby constitutes the maximum rent. The amount of the charge, therefore, whether measured by meter or otherwise, is considered a part of the rent paid by the tenant for the use and occupancy of the premises and for the services furnished in connection therewith. 

The landlord may therefore proceed in the following manner. A landlord who wishes to discontinue his present submetering practice and desires to give unlimited electrical current to tenants under a rent inclusion plan may file an application pursuant to Section 35(1) requesting permis sion of the Local Rent Administrator to discontinue furnishing metered electrical current, and for permission to remove the meters in the individual apartments. One master application may be filed with sufficient additional copies to be served on the tenants. The application should state that the landlord will furnish unmetered current in return for the establishment of the maximum rent at a fixed amount, instead of the varying amount now in effect. The landlord must submit evidence as to the actual experienced 12-month electric bill for each apartment. The landlord must also submit evidence of the actual experienced 12month electric bill for each type or class of apartment. 

It is suggested that the application be prepared so as to contain the following items: 1. type of apartment (1, 2 or 3 

rooms) and the designation of each apartment affected; 2. the base legal maximum rent exclusive of any electric charge; 3. average of the actual experienced 12-month electric bill for that apartment; 4. the average of the actual experienced 12-month electric bill for that type or class 339

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

of apartment; 5. the total rent requested which will include the base rent and the amount for unlimited electric service. 

The Local Rent Administrator may then issue an order granting permission to the landlord to change from furnishing electrical current on a metered basis to an unmetered basis, granting permission to remove the meters and establishing the maximum rent at a fixed and definite amount each month. This new maximum rent would include the previous base rent for each apartment plus the amount designated in item 4 above.

However, having given due regard to the equities involved affecting tenants in possession on May 1, 1952, the State Rent Administrator has determined that such order will also establish an alternate maximum rent which will include the previous base rent for such apartment plus the average of the actual experienced 12-month bills for that apartment, and such alternate maximum rent will be in effect so long as that tenant remains in possession. 

If the landlord requests an increase in excess of the fixed maximum rent to be determined in the manner described in the foregoing paragraph upon the assumption that the furnishing of unmetered electric current to tenants may result in substantial increases in electrical consumption, he must obtain the written consents of tenants to such an increase and attach them to his application. The reasonableness of the increase requested in excess of the amount as established by the procedure in the preceding paragraph will be determined by the Local Rent Administrator on the basis of the information, facts and figures submitted by the landlord in his application. If the landlord succeeds in securing the written consent of tenants in occupancy of at least 75% of the housing accommodations in the structure, the consent of the minority of the tenants would not be required and the Local Rent Administrator could issue 340

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

orders granting the increases as stated above affecting all of the housing accommodations in the structure. If the landlord does not secure the written consents of at least 75% of the tenants in the structure, then orders granting such increases would be issued only against those tenants who have consented. Tenants who have not consented would have their rent fixed merely on the basis of the procedure outlined in the preceding paragraph. 

No landlord can be required to adopt a rent inclusion plan. A landlord who desires to adopt a rent inclusion plan must make it available to all his tenants. A tenant who does not desire to participate in a rent inclusion plan may make his own arrangement directly with the utility company. However, the landlord must give the utility company serving the area any written authorization which may be required and must also permit the tenant to use the present electrical wiring and to install any additional wiring which may be required at the tenant's own expense.

§ 36-Order Issued by O.H.E. Between March 1, 1949 and March 1, 1950 Based upon Additional Services and Facilities. Administrator's Opin ion No. 21 (For New York City only).

The Local Rent Administrator will determine the maximum rent pursuant to Section 36 of the Regulations. A housing accommodation within the City of New York had a maximum rent of $50.00 per month on March 1, 1949.
