ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS PART V-EVICTIONS Part V-Eviction by Municipality Because of Violation of Local Ordinances. Administrator's Opinion No. 38. 

Certain housing accommodations occupied by a tenant are in violation of local ordinances because of their unsanitary condition and the fact that they are not fit for human habitation as housing accommodations. Pursuant to such local ordinances, the municipality desires to take legal steps to have the tenant removed because of such conditions. Is the eviction of the tenant by the municipality subject to the Rent and Eviction Regulations?

Since the person seeking the eviction of the tenant is not the landlord of the housing accommodation but is a municipality proceeding under local ordinances, the Temporary State Housing Rent Commission will not interfere or intervene in the proceeding to remove the tenant. 

Section 51. Restrictions on removal of tenant including hotel tenants. 

§ 51(2)-Self-Created Necessity-Additional Occupants. See Administrator's Opinion No. 69 Under Section 55(1). 

§ 51(2)—Attempt to Evict Tenant Who Occupies Residential Housing Accommodations Under Commercial Lease Executed As a Device to Evade the Rent Regulations. Administrator's Opinion No. 102. 

An apartment has a registered maximum rent of $61.00 per month. When it becomes vacant the landlord rents it by commercial lease in the sum of $85.00 per month, the lease reciting that the tenant is to practice his profession as a podiatrist in the apartment. Actually the tenant practices his profession as a podiatrist in another building and uses the accommodations from the very inception of his tenancy exclusively for residential purposes. The tenant becomes aware of the registered maximum rent of $61.00 344

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

per month and complains either to the landlord, or to the 

Local Rent Office which then notifies the landlord. 

The landlord thereafter attempts to evict the tenant pur suant to Section 52(1) of the Regulations upon the ground that the tenant is violating a substantial obligation of his tenancy by not using the apartment for the practice of his profession. The tenant asserts that the landlord knew from the inception of his lease that he would use it only for housing purposes and that this attempt to evict him is retaliatory because he has asserted his rights under the 

Rent Regulations. May the tenant be evicted? The device of using a commercial lease agreement in order to secure more than the maximum rent for the apartment will not deprive the tenant of his rights under the Regulations. Therefore, if a court finds the facts to be as stated by the tenant he may not be evicted since Section 

10(2) of the Emergency Housing Rent Control Law, as amended, and Section 51(2) of the Regulations, specifically prohibit the eviction of any tenant where the attempt to evict the tenant is made ". because such tenant or occupant has taken, or proposes to take action authorized or required by the Act or any regulations, order or requirement thereunder." The tenant was within his legal rights in refusing to pay more than the maximum legal rent of the accommodations since, on the facts, this commercial lease was an attempt to evade the residential rent ceilings. 

Consequently he may not now be evicted upon the ground that he is violating a substantial obligation of his tenancy. 

Pursuant to Section 14 of the Regulations, the Court can find that the provision of the lease under which the landlord is attempting to evict the tenant cannot be enforced as it is inconsistent with the Act and these Regulations. 

The signing of the commercial lease by the tenant cannot be deemed a waiver of his rights since Section 16 of the 345

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

Regulations specifically provides that an agreement by the tenant to waive the benefit of any provision of the Act or the Regulations is void. 

Section 52. Proceedings for eviction without certificate.. 

§ 52-Preliminary Notice Required in Cases of Violation of Substantial Obligation of Tenancy. Administrator's Opinion No. 64. 

A landlord served a notice upon his tenant to vacate a housing accommodation alleging that the tenant has violated a substantial obligation of his tenancy as set forth in Section 52(1) of the Regulations. Within forty-eight hours, an exact copy of this notice, together with an affidavit of service, was filed with the Local Rent Office in accordance with the requirements of Section 53. The tenant complained to the Local Rent Office that he had not received any previous notice from the landlord that he was violating a substantial obligation of his tenancy and that he was given no previous opportunity to cease the violation. Has the landlord complied with all the requirements of Sections 

52 and 53 of the Regulations?

No. The landlord has not complied with the requirements of Section 52 and, therefore, may not now commence a court action to remove or evict the tenant. Sections 52(1) and 53 provide for two successive notices. The first must be a preliminary notice under Section 52(1) to inform the tenant of the violation and to afford him an opportunity of curing the same. If the tenant has not cured the violation within the time period set forth in this first notice which must be at least 10 days, the landlord may then serve the notice to vacate required under Section 53. The only exception to this requirement of a preliminary notice in such a case is where "within the three-month period immediately prior to the commencement of the proceeding, the tenant 346

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

has wilfully violated such an obligation, inflicting serious and substantial injury to the landlord." § 52 Tenant Fails to Vacate After Notifying Landlord of His Intention to Do So. See Administrator's Opinion No. 71 Under Section 54. 

§ 52(3)—Eviction of Tenants because Building Violations Are Placed Against the Premises. Administrator's Opinion No. 114. 

The Administrator has received numerous requests for advice from landlords as to whether in the following situations they may evict tenants by proceeding directly in the Municipal Court under Section 52(3) of the Rent and Evic tion Regulations: 

(1) Where the Department of Buildings has placed vio lations against the building because it does not contain an adequate number of water closets; (2) Where the Department of Buildings has placed violations against the building because needed repairs, painting and maintenance have not been provided; (3) Where the Department of Buildings has placed violations against the building because of a failure to secure approval of alterations or to file necessary plans. 

In some instances, landlords claimed that the financial expenditure necessary to remove the violations is not warranted and, therefore, instead of spending money to cure the illegal condition, they wish to evict the tenants in order to board up or demolish the building. In other cases, landlord claimed that in order to remove the violations, it is necessary that the tenants surrender possession of their apartments. However, the landlords intend to rerent the apartments after the alterations are completed. 

In the opinion of the Administrator, a landlord may not proceed directly in the Municipal Court under Section 52(3) to evict tenants in any of the above three situations. Section 52(3) of the Regulations, derived from Section 5(c) of the Act, permits the eviction of tenants where "occupancy 347

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

of the housing accommodation by the tenant is illegal because of the requirements of law, and the landlord is subject to civil or criminal penalties therefor or both.” 

The existence of Department of Buildings violations does not necessarily make the tenant's occupancy illegal. Both the statutory and administrative intent was to authorize the eviction of a tenant to cure a building violation only where the tenant's occupancy is itself the violation of law (e.g., where a third tenant occupies an illegal basement or attic space in a two-family house and the accommodation cannot be legalized by a reasonable expenditure of money). In that circumstance, it is the tenant's occupancy of the space as a housing accommodation which is illegal and causes the violation. In the three situations enumerated above, the violations did not result from, nor are they related to the tenant's occupancy of the apartment. These violations have resulted solely from the landlord's failure to complete installations, alterations or repairs to the building as required by law. Therefore, Section 52(3) would not be applicable.

A landlord may proceed under Section 52(3) where the tenant's occupancy of the housing accommodation is illegal (e.g., where a third tenant occupies an illegal basement or attic space in a two-family house and the accommodation cannot be legalized by a reasonable expenditure of money), but only if the landlord intends to discontinue permanently the housing use of the space. Under no circumstance may a landlord proceed under that section where he seeks to discontinue temporarily the use of the housing accommodation in order to remove violations and thereafter rerent. 

Even if the required alterations cannot be safely completed with the tenants remaining in possession, the landlord may not evict them but may make application to the Local 

Rent Administrator for their temporary removal. Where 348

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

warranted, relief will be granted under Section 54 and appropriate conditions imposed. Where a landlord seeks to withdraw permanently an entire building from the rental market, he may obtain certificates of eviction under Section 59(2) upon establishing his compliance with the conditions therein provided.

Section 53. Notices required in proceedings under Section 

52. 

§ 53—Notice as Affecting Requirements of Real Property Law. Administrator's Opinion No. 49. 

A landlord wishes to evict his tenant upon the ground that the tenant is using the housing accommodation for an immoral or illegal purpose. The tenant is a month to month tenant. The landlord wishes to know whether he must give the tenant a thirty-day notice required by Section 232 of the Real Property Law in addition to the ten-day notice prescribed by Section 53. 

Section 53 of the Regulations does not change Section 232 of the Real Property Law. Section 53 merely sets forth minimum notice requirements under the Regulations and does not affect the tenant's term of occupancy under his rental agreement. The service of the thirty-day notice is required by State law in order to terminate a month to month or monthly tenancy. The obligation to give this notice and the manner of serving said notice are not affected by Section 53. However, the landlord may incorporate the requirements of Section 53 in his thirty-day notice. He is under no obligation to serve separate notices. 

§ 53—Preliminary Notice Required in Cases of Violation of Substantial Obligation of Tenancy. See Administrator's Opinion No. 64 Under Section 52. 

§ 53—Landlord Claims Occupant to Be a Squatter. See Administrator's Opinion No. 57 Under Section 56. 

349

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

Section 54. Proceeding for eviction with certificate. 

§ 54-Discretionary Certificates. Administrator's Opinion No. 3. 

The Local Rent Administrator has the authority to issue certificates of eviction under this section in cases not specifically covered under Sections 55 to 59, inclusive, provided he finds that the requested removal or eviction is not inconsistent with the purposes of the Act or of the Regulations, and would not be likely to result in the circumvention or evasion thereof.

This discretion must be exercised very carefully. The issuance of a certificate establishes a precedent, and consistency of administration would require that a certificate be issued in every other case of the same class, where application is made, since in effect, a new ground of eviction would be added to those specifically enumerated in the Regulations. 

The Local Rent Administrator must consider whether evictions in that class of cases are inconsistent with the purposes of the Act or Regulations, or would be likely to result in the circumvention or evasion thereof. 

Administrator's Opinions Nos. 4, 5, 6, and 7 cite cases in which it would be proper to issue certificates pursuant to this section.

Every certificate must contain the waiting period specified in Section 54(2). 

§ 54-Occupancy by a Superintendent. Administrator's Opinion No. 4. 

A landlord files an application to evict a tenant in order to secure housing accommodations for a superintendent. 

Would it be proper to grant such an application?

It is necessary for the landlord to establish good faith and an immediate and compelling necessity to have a resident superintendent upon the premises. 350

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

Immediate and compelling necessity is established if the landlord is required by law to have a resident superintendent. If not so required, immediate and compelling necessity may be established if the landlord is unable, for a proper reason, either to take care of the building himself or to secure a non-resident superintendent.

The issue of good faith may be resolved by inquiring into why the landlord seeks to install a resident superintendent in those cases where no violation has as yet been issued or where no resident superintendent is required by law. The landlord complies with the good faith requirement where he establishes his honest intention and desires to gain possession of the apartment for occupancy by a resident superintendent.

However, where the landlord chooses a particular apartment for occupancy by a resident superintendent because the tenant of that apartment has been active in asserting his rights under the Rent Regulations and has therefore incurred the ill will of his landlord, such tenant may be protected against eviction since Section 10(2) of the Emergency Housing Rent Control Law, as amended, which is incorporated in Section 51(2) of the Regulations, specifically prohibits the eviction of any tenant where the attempt to evict the tenant is made ". . because such tenant or occupant has taken, or proposes to take action authorized or required by the Act or any regulations, order or requirement thereunder." Whether or not the motivation of the application for a certificate is predominately retaliatory or not is a question of fact. For example, inquiry should be made into whether the particular apartment sought had previously been occupied by a resident superintendent or whether there were any other special reasons, such as location, which make this apartment the logical one to be occupied by a resident 351

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

superintendent. The basic question to be determined is whether the predominant motive for evicting the tenant is retaliation because the tenant had asserted his rights under the Rent Regulations or the necessity of the landlord to install a resident superintendent in that particular apartment. This issue of retaliation is separate and distinct from the issue of good faith. $54-Company Housing Occupied by Non-Employee-Tenant. Adminis trator's Opinion No. 5. 

A manufacturing company owns housing accommodations which are rented for and occupied exclusively by employees of the company for the mutual convenience of both parties as part of the employment agreement. One of the tenants has ceased to be an employee, and the company therefore wishes to evict this tenant in order to permit another employee to occupy the premises. May a certificate of eviction be issued?

Where it is clear that the landlord requires a housing accommodation now occupied by a former employee in order to house an employee, the Local Rent Administrator may, in his discretion, issue a certificate of eviction. 

§ 54-Company Housing Occupied by Non-Employee-Tenant. Administrator's Opinion No. 6. 

A manufacturing company owns several dwelling units constructed for, and subsequently occupied exclusively by employees of the company as part of the employment agreement. The company suspended operations and the vacated accommodations were rented to non-employee tenants. At the present time the company intends to resume operations and wishes to evict the tenants in order to put new employees in occupancy. May the company obtain certificates of eviction? 

Certificates of eviction may, in the discretion of the 352

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

Local Rent Administrator, be issued authorizing the eviction of the tenants, where it appears that the tenants are occupying housing which is part of a company housing development in which occupancy, prior to the closing of the plant, had been limited to employees of the landlord and that the accommodations are essential to house new employees. 

§ 54—Self-Occupancy by a Landlord for Professional Purposes. Administrator's Opinion No. 7. 

A dentist is being evicted from the office he now occupies. 

He therefore desires to evict a tenant from housing accommodations owned by him so that he may practice dentistry on the premises. May a certificate of eviction be issued? 

The Local Rent Administrator may, in his discretion, issue a certificate in a case of this sort where it appears that these are exceptional circumstances and that the landlord has an immediate and compelling necessity to recover possession of the housing accommodations and where it further appears that the landlord has no intention of renting the unit for either housing or non-housing purposes or any intent to sell the housing accommodations. To deny a certificate would, therefore, be to impose an undue hardship upon him.

A similar approach is proper where possession is sought for a member of the family. For example, if the landlord's son was a dentist who was being evicted from his office quarters, or had no office quarters and could not obtain them, it would be proper to apply similar criteria to the landlord's application. 

§ 54-Eviction of Sub-Lessee. Administrator's Opinion No. 28. 

The tenant of an apartment sub-leased it for the summer months with the explicit understanding that the sub-tenant 

—23 

3.5.3

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

would vacate on September 30. The sub-tenant refuses to vacate. May the tenant obtain a certificate of eviction against his sub-tenant?

Yes. The issuance of a certificate would not be inconsistent with the purposes of the Act or the Regulations. 

The Local Rent Administrator, in his discretion, may waive all or part of the 3-month waiting period. [See Adminis trator's Opinion No. 26 under Section 33(1)]. 

§ 54-Claim by Tenant of Unexpired Rental Agreement. Administrator's Opinion No. 29. 

A landlord files an application for a certificate of eviction. 

The tenant's answer disputes his right to file an application, on the grounds that there is a rental agreement still in effect which has not terminated. Should the landlord's application be processed?

A decision as to whether or not a certificate should be issued should be based solely upon the relevant provisions of the Regulations. We will not determine whether or not there are existing leases, automatic renewals, and extensions of leases. These are questions which should be resolved by the local courts. However, where the landlord seeks a certificate for immediate use and occupancy, and there is clearly a written lease agreement in existence which still has a long time to run, the certificate may be denied, upon the theory that the landlord is not seeking in good faith to recover possession for his immediate use and occupancy. 354

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS § 54 Self-Occupancy Where Landlord is Willing to Relocate Tenant. 

Administrator's Opinion No. 50 [Revoked. See Administrator's Opinion No. 118 Under Section 54]. 

§ 54—Eviction for Self-Use by Hospital. Administrator's Opinion No. 54 [Revoked]. 

§ 54 Occupancy by Widow and Children of Deceased Son of Landlord. 

Administrator's Opinion No. 62. 

A landlord files an application for a certificate of eviction for the use and occupancy of the widow and children of his deceased son. The landlord is proceeding in good faith and has established that there is an immediate and compelling necessity for occupancy by the widow. May a certificate of eviction be issued, and if so, under what section of the Regulations?

In view of the definition of "immediate family" contained in Section 55(1) of the Regulations which limits the relationship to that of son, daughter, father or mother, no certificate of eviction may be issued under that section. 

However, since the certificate of eviction would have been issued had the son been alive, it would not now be contrary to the purpose and intent of the Act and the Regulations to grant a certificate under Section 54 of the Regulations for the purpose of occupancy by the family of the deceased son. 

§ 54 Tenant Fails to Vacate After Notifying Landlord of His Intention to Do So. Administrator's Opinion No. 71. 

A tenant gave his landlord written notice that he would vacate his apartment on a certain date. The landlord in reliance upon the tenant's notice rerented the housing accommodations to a third party effective as of the date on which the tenant had stated he would vacate. The tenant, however, thereafter failed to vacate. May the landlord commence court action to evict the tenant without first obtaining a certificate of eviction? 

No. A court may not take jurisdiction of any eviction 3.55

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

action based upon such facts unless the landlord first obtains a certificate of eviction from the Local Rent Administrator. This cannot be considered the type of case falling within the meaning of Section 52(1) of the Regulations since that section specifically excepts from its provisions the violation of an "obligation to surrender possession of such housing accommodations." Nor is there any other ground under Section 52 upon which the landlord may rely for proceedings of eviction without first obtaining a certificate. The mere fact that the tenant notified the landlord of his intention to vacate does not remove the tenant from the protection of the Rent and Eviction Regulations. 

However, where the tenant has given the landlord written notice of his intention to move on a specified date, and the landlord in reliance upon such notice rerents to a third party effective as of the date on which the tenant had agreed to vacate, it would not be inconsistent with the purposes and intent of the Act and the Regulations to issue a certificate of eviction under Section 54 of the Regulations where the inability of the landlord to give possession to the new tenant will result in substantial damage and hardship to the landlord.

Section 54(2) of the Regulations authorizes the Admin istrator to waive a part or all of the waiting period where the applicant demonstrates that undue hardship would result because of the imposition of such waiting period. The imposition of a waiting period in such a case would result in undue hardship to the landlord and a certificate will be issued without any waiting period. 

§ 54-Eviction of Sub-Tenant by Serviceman-Tenant. 

Opinion No. 72. 

Administrator's

A member of the Armed Forces was assigned to active duty and sub-leased his apartment with the express under- 356

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

standing that at such time as he or his wife desired occupancy of the housing accommodations, the sub-tenant would vacate.

The serviceman or his wife now desires to regain possession of the housing accommodations. The sub-tenant refuses to vacate after receipt of a 30-day notice to vacate. 

May a certificate of eviction be issued against the sub-tenant without any waiting period?

Yes. A certificate of eviction may be issued under Section 54 of the Rent and Eviction Regulations since the tenant who put the sub-tenant in possession and now seeks his eviction is considered a landlord within the meaning of that section of the Regulations. Such an eviction would not be inconsistent with the purposes and intent of the act and the Regulations. Section 54(2) of the Regulations authorizes the Administrator to waive a part or all of the waiting period where the applicant demonstrates that undue hardship would result because of the imposition of such waiting period. The imposition of a waiting period in such a case would constitute undue hardship within the meaning of that provision of the Regulations. Therefore, a certificate of eviction will be issued immediately without any waiting period.

The foregoing opinion would also apply to the renting of a home owned and occupied by a serviceman under similar circumstances. 

(See Administrator's Opinion No. 28 under Section 54.) § 54-Certificates of Eviction Where Landlord Offers to Exchange Apartments or to Relocate Tenant. Administrator's Opinion No. 118. 

A landlord resides in an apartment which is comparable in all substantial respects to the apartment occupied by another tenant. He now files an application for a Certificate of Eviction against that tenant and claims that he need not 357

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

establish the existence of an immediate and compelling necessity but only evidence of his good faith. As evidence of his good faith, he offers his own apartment for occupancy by the tenant and, in addition, offers to pay for the tenant's moving expenses and to redecorate the apartment. Will the 

Certificate of Eviction be granted?

Yes. Since the tenant is not being deprived of living quarters and is being offered the opportunity of occupying an apartment which is comparable to his present accommodations in all substantial respects, the landlord need not establish the existence of an immediate and compelling necessity. However, the landlord must be acting in good faith in seeking to exchange apartments and must not be using it merely as a device to evict a tenant who cannot accept the landlord's apartment. In granting Certificates of Eviction in such cases the Local Rent Administrator may require the landlord to meet reasonable conditions in order to demonstrate his good faith. The maximum rent will be fixed by the Local Rent Administrator. Conditions may be imposed which will alleviate any hardship which the tenant may suffer as a result of the landlord's request. The landlord may be required to reimburse the tenant for his moving expenses or any other reasonable expenses which the tenant may have incurred as well as to redecorate the apartment which is offered to the tenant. 

The conditions which the Local Rent Administrator may impose will depend upon the facts of each individual case. 

The rental shall be fixed as follows: (a) if there is an established maximum rent, the rental shall be fixed by the 

Local Rent Administrator after giving due consideration. to such established maximum rent and all other factors bearing upon the equities involved, or (b) if there is no established maximum rent, at a rental to be mutually agreed upon by the landlord and tenant or, in the absence of such 358

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

agreement, to be fixed by the Local Rent Administrator as fair and equitable under all the circumstances. 

This revision follows the decision by the Supreme Court in Johnson v. Weaver (Sup. Ct. Bronx Co., Fine, J., New 

York Law Journal, June 21, 1957, p. 8, cols. 1 and 2).

Further, the apartment to be occupied by the tenant will continue to remain subject to the Rent and Eviction Regulations as long as this tenant remains in occupancy. The apartment which was formerly occupied by the landlord is not decontrolled under either Section 2(2)(h) of the Act 

[Section 9(11) of the Regulations] or Section 2(2)(i) of the Act [Section 9(12) of the Regulations] since it was not the Legislative intent to grant decontrol in cases where the vacancy was created by the exchange of apartments with a tenant for the landlord's convenience. The tenant occupied a controlled housing accommodation and the landlord's former apartment is a mere substitution of controlled housing accommodations.

Where the landlord offers in good faith to exchange apartments with the tenant or to relocate him to accommodations in the same building or in another building owned or under the landlord's control, a certificate of eviction will be issued permitting such exchange or relocation since the conditions above specified can be imposed upon the landlord. In such a situation the tenant will be moving from one controlled accommodation to another controlled accommodation. The issuance of a certificate of eviction would therefore not be inconsistent with the intent and purposes of the Act and the Regulations and the tenant could properly be required to accept the landlord's offer. 

On the other hand where the offer of exchange or relocation would compel the tenant to accept a decontrolled accommodation it would not be unreasonable for the tenant 359

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

to refuse the landlord's offer and no certificate of eviction would be issued.

Section 55. Occupancy by landlord or immediate family. 

§ 55(1) Certificate of Eviction for Occupancy by Father-in-Law or Mother-in-Law. Administrator's Opinion No. 41 [Revoked]. 

§ 55(1)-Service of Notice to Vacate Prior to Expiration of Waiting Period in Certificate Case. Administrator's Opinion No. 51. 

A landlord has obtained a certificate of eviction for selfoccupancy, effective three months from the date of its issuance. Since the tenant is a month to month tenant, he is required by the Real Property Law to serve a thirty-day notice of termination of tenancy. May the landlord serve this notice prior to the termination of the three-month period set forth in the certificate of eviction?

Yes. The Regulations permit the service of such a notice. This is not considered to be the commencement of proceedings to remove or evict a tenant within the meaning of the Regulations. However, the notice must not require the tenant to surrender possession prior to the expiration of the waiting period set forth in the certificate. 

$55(1)-Self-Occupancy by Sole Stockholder of Landlord Corporation. 

Administrator's Opinion No. 60. 

An application for a certificate of eviction was filed by the sole stockholder of a landlord corporation. He seeks to evict a tenant for occupancy by his son. The apartment is located in a building consisting of 20 housing accommodations. May a certificate of eviction be granted under 

Section 55 of the Rent and Eviction Regulations? 

No. Section 55(1) provides that a certificate may be issued to a landlord "for his own personal use and occupancy or for the use and occupancy of his immediate family" provided certain other requirements are met. Since the landlord of the accommodations is a corporation, there can 360

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

be "no personal use and occupancy" for the immediate family of a corporation. Therefore, a certificate of eviction may not be issued in such a case, nor may a certificate be issued where the sole stockholder desires the eviction of the tenant for his own personal use and occupancy. 

§ 55(1)—Jurisdiction of the Court After Issuance of Certificate of Eviction. Administrator's Opinion No. 63. 

A certificate of eviction was issued to a landlord for personal occupancy of an apartment. The landlord brought summary proceedings to evict the tenant. The tenant then submitted proof to the Court that after the issuance of the eviction certificate, the property had been advertised for sale, specifying a vacant apartment as an attractive feature to the buyer, and the tenant asked the Court to find that the landlord did not in fact intend personally to occupy the apartment, and to dismiss the summary proceedings. 

The landlord contended that the Court was bound by the certificate of eviction issued by the Commission, and could not accept proof of the advertisement, even though it occurred after the Commission had issued its certificate. 

May the Court accept the proof, and in a proper case, deny a final order of eviction on the facts occurring after the Commission's issuance of the certificate? 

The Appellate Division, 1st Dept., in the case of Spotless 

Stores, Inc. v. Smith, 107 N.Y.S. 2d 734, held that while the Court could not deny a final order of eviction after a certificate of eviction had been issued by the Commission, the Court could refer the matter back to the Rent Administrator for a determination as to whether or not the certificate of eviction should be revoked. The Court said: "On a prima facie showing of intention to abandon such purpose the Municipal Court should stay the trial of any summary proceeding for a reasonable time in order to allow 361

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

the tenant to apply to the Rent Commission for revocation of the certificate of eviction. The emergency statute and the rules adopted thereunder would appear to give the Rent Administrator power to entertain an application for revocation and allow him to waive any time limitation contained in the rules with respect thereto, upon good cause shown. 

Certainly an abandonment of the basic intention or original purpose of the landlord occurring after expiration of the usual time for protest or review would constitute good cause for waiver of any limitation of time. And such abandonment would justify revocation, if established."

Upon remission by the Court the Commission will reopen the matter and determine whether the certificate of eviction should be permitted to remain in full force or whether it should be revoked.

Upon facts similar to those set forth above, but where no court proceedings have as yet been instituted, the Commission would have power to reopen the proceedings to determine whether the certificate should remain in effect or be revoked. Section 54(4) of the Regulations provides: 

"4. In the event that the landlord's intentions or circumstances so change that the premises, possession of which is sought, will not be used for the purpose specified in the certificate, the certificate shall thereupon be null and void. 

The landlord shall immediately notify the Local Rent Administrator in writing and surrender the certificate for cancellation." 

§ 55(1)-Existence of Vacant Apartment Prior to Filing of Application. 

Administrator's Opinion No. 67. 

A landlord of a house containing more than two apartments files an application for a certificate of eviction and establishes that he has an immediate and compelling necessity and that he seeks in good faith to evict the tenant for 362

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

self-occupancy. However, the tenant claims that six months ago there was a vacant apartment in this building into which the landlord could have moved and, therefore, urges that the certificate should not be granted. Should a certificate of eviction be granted or denied?

An inquiry will be made into the reason why the landlord failed to take possession of the vacant apartment. The landlord must establish some reasonable basis for failing to relieve his housing needs at that time. If the vacant apartment was reasonably suitable and adequate 'for his needs, and if his housing requirements were similar to those which he now has, the Local Rent Administrator may draw the conclusion that his failure to avail himself of that apartment warrants the inference that there was and is no immediate compelling necessity warranting the eviction of the tenant.

However, if the landlord can establish some reasonable basis for his failure to take the vacant apartment, such as a change in his housing needs, after the date of the vacancy, this failure to avail himself of the vacant apartment would not bar him from proving his present requirements. On the issue of good faith, the Local Rent Administrator may also inquire into whether, even if the apartment was not suitable to the landlord, he should not have offered to exchange it for the apartment occupied by the tenant. In each case the Local Rent Administrator will make the factual determination as to whether the landlord has established that he seeks in good faith to recover possession for self-occupancy or for occupancy by members of his immediate family and the existence of an immediate and compelling necessity to recover possession of such housing accommodations.

Where the apartment which the landlord seeks to occupy is located in a one- or two-family house, an immediate and $363

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

compelling necessity need not be established. However, the landlord's failure to take a vacant apartment under the circumstances outlined above may indicate a lack of good faith warranting denial of the application for a certificate of eviction. 

§ 55(1) Self-Created Necessity-Sale of Housing Accommodations by Landlord. Administrator's Opinion No. 68. 

A landlord of a house containing more than two housing accommodations files an application for a certificate of eviction. He establishes that the housing accommodations now occupied by him are clearly inadequate and that he has an immediate and compelling necessity. He further establishes that the sole purpose for which he seeks to dispossess the tenant is for self occupancy. The tenant claims that the certificate of eviction should not be granted because the landlord had recently sold housing accommodations owned by him in which he had been residing comfortably in order to make a profit. The tenant, therefore, claims that the landlord's need is self created and that the certificate must be denied. Should an application be granted or denied on these facts?

Section 5(2)(a) of the Emergency Housing Rent Control 

Law, as amended, specifically provides that the landlord must establish two requirements in order to obtain a certificate: (1) that he seeks in good faith to recover possession of such housing accommodations for his own personal use and occupancy or for the use and occupancy of his immediate family; and (2) that he establish the existence of an immediate and compelling necessity where the accommodations are located in a house containing more than two housing accommodations. Section 55(1) of the Rent and 

Eviction Regulations embodies the same requirements as are contained in the Act.. 364

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

The Administrator has accepted the reasoning of our Court of Appeals which, in the case of Rosenbluth v Finkelstein et al, 300 NY 402, 91 NE2d 581, interpreting a similar requirement reversed the Temporary City Rent Commission, stating: 

"(2) Quite obviously, the Commission either added a new requirement to the Statute or misconstrued the term 'good faith' as there used. Issuance of a certificate, eviction of a tenant, is sanctioned at the hands of a landlord who 'seeks in good faith to recover possession' of dwelling accommodations for his own immediate occupancy. The words mean precisely what they say, and the Commission may not under the guise of administering the Statute ascribe a different or unreasonable meaning to those words. The landlord complies with the Statute's demands if he seeks the eviction with the honest intention and desire to gain possession of the premises for his own use. See H. Kauffman & Sons Saddlery Co. v Miller, 298 NY 38, 43, 80 NE2d 322, 324; see also 

Nofree v Leonard, 327 Ill App 143, 147, 63 NE2d 653. "(3) It is indisputable that the petitioner wanted and sought the apartment for the use of himself and his family. 

There is not the slightest proof or even suggestion that he was motivated by any intent other than to gain a place in which to live. The mere fact that he agreed that his vendor might remain in possession of another apartment in the building does not permit the inference of fraud or bad faith and no fiat or policy pronouncement by the Commission can add the one or supply the other. In short, there just is no evidence to cast doubt upon petitioner's bona fides. The 

Commission's determination denying a certificate of eviction was, therefore, without basis or warrant, and decisions such as Park East Land Corp. v Finkelstein, 299 NY 70, 85 NE2d 869, have no application." 365

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

The fact that the landlord has previously sold housing accommodations owned and occupied by him does not in and of itself raise any presumption that he is proceeding in bad faith. It, therefore, follows that where a landlord has established (1) that he seeks in good faith to recover possession for self occupancy or for occupancy by members of his immediate family, and (2) the existence of an immediate and compelling necessity to recover possession of the housing accommodations, a certificate of eviction must be granted. 

Where the housing accommodations which the landlord seeks to occupy are located in a one- or two-family house, an immediate and compelling necessity need not be established. 

$55(1) Self-Created Necessity—Additional Occupants. Administrator's Opinion No. 69. 

A landlord of a house containing more than two housing accommodations files an application for a certificate of eviction and establishes that within his four room apartment there are residing eight persons, and that therefore he has an immediate compelling necessity to recover possession of the housing accommodations occupied by this tenant. 

The tenant concedes that there are eight persons now residing in the landlord's apartment, and further concedes that the landlord does intend to use the housing accommodations for the housing needs of himself and his family. 

However, the tenant points out that the landlord has taken into his present apartment a sister-in-law and her three minor children. The tenant, therefore, urges that the overcrowded condition of the landlord's present apartment has been created by the landlord, and that, therefore, the certificate should be denied. The landlord explains that his brother died recently and that he, therefore, has had to 366

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

assist his sister-in-law by inviting her to live with him. 

Should a certificate of eviction be granted or denied?

The fact that the landlord permitted additional persons to become members of his family does not in and of itself raise any presumption that he is proceeding in bad faith. 

The Administrator has accepted the reasoning of our Court of Appeals which, in the case of Rosenbluth v Finkelstein, et al, 300 NY 402, 91 NE2d 581, interpreting a similar requirement reversed the Temporary City Rent Commission, stating: "(2) Quite obviously, the Commission either added a new requirement to the Statute or misconstrued the term 'good faith' as there used. Issuance of a certificate, eviction of a tenant, is sanctioned at the hands of a landlord who 'seeks in good faith to recover possession' of dwelling accommodations for his own immediate occupancy. The words mean precisely what they say, and the Commission may not under the guise of administering the Statute ascribe a different or unreasonable meaning to those words. The landlord complies with the Statute's demands if he seeks the eviction with the honest intention and desire to gain possession of the premises for his own use. See H. Kauffman & Sons 

Saddlery Co. v Miller, 298 NY 38, 43, 80 NE2d 322, 324; see also Nofree v Leonard, 327 Ill App 143, 147, 63 NE2d 653. "(3) It is indisputable that the petitioner wanted and sought the apartment for the use of himself and his family. 

There is not the slightest proof or even suggestion that he was motivated by any intent other than to gain a place in which to live. The mere fact that he agreed that his vendor might remain in possession of another apartment in the building does not permit the inference of fraud or bad faith and no fiat or policy pronouncement by the Commission can add the one or supply the other. In short, there just is no evidence to cast doubt upon the petitioner's bona 367

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

fides. The Commission's determination denying a certificate of eviction was, therefore, without basis or warrant, and decisions such as Park East Land Corp. v Finkelstein, 299 

NY 70, 85 NE2d 869, have no application."

The inquiry of the Local Rent Administrator will, therefore, normally be confined to the question of whether or not the landlord has established that his present family is inadequately housed and that, therefore, he has the immediate and compelling necessity required by the Regulations. Inquiry would be proper as to whether the sisterin-law and her children have become and will remain a permanent part of the landlord's family, or whether the arrangement is a temporary one which will be terminated in the reasonably near future.

Should any of the occupants of the landlord's housing accommodations consist of roomers, the Local Rent Administrator may détermine that the landlord may relieve his housing necessity by requiring the roomers to move, and that, therefore, there is no immediate and compelling necessity warranting the eviction of the tenant. 

The Local Rent Administrator may further inquire into whether the landlord has permitted additional occupants into his housing accommodations as a device to evict the tenant because the tenant has taken, or proposes to take, action authorized or required by the Rent and Eviction 

Regulations. Retaliatory evictions are banned by Sections 51(2) of the Regulations and Section 10(2) of the Emergency Housing Rent Control Law, as amended. 

However, should the Local Rent Administrator find that the landlord is proceeding in good faith, and that he has an immediate and compelling necessity for the larger housing accommodations occupied by his tenant, he may not deny the certificate on the ground that the landlord has created. 368

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

his own hardship.

Where the housing accommodations which the landlord seeks to occupy, are located in a oneor two-family house, an immediate and compelling necessity need not be established. 

§ 55(1)—Foster Child as Member of Landlord's "Immediate Family." Administrator's Opinion No. 78. 

A landlord files an application for a certificate of eviction pursuant to Section 55(1) of the Rent and Eviction Regu lations to recover possession of housing accommodations because of immediate and compelling necessity for the use and occupancy of the accommodations by his foster daughter, the child of his wife by a former marriage. Is the foster daughter a member of the landlord's "immediate family" within the meaning of that section of the Regulations? 

Section 55(1) of the Regulations restricts the term "immediate family" to a "son, daughter, father, mother, fatherin-law or mother-in-law," only, of the landlord. A foster child is not considered a son or daughter within the meaning of that definition unless the son or daughter has been legally adopted by the landlord. $55(1) Occupancy by Widow and Children of Deceased Son of Land lord. See Administrator's Opinion No. 62 Under Section 54. 

§ 55(1)-Certificate of Eviction Where a Landlord is Already in Occupancy of One Apartment in a Two-Family House and Seeks Possession of the Other Apartment Also. Administrator's Opinion No. 82. 

A landlord resides with his family on the second floor of a two-family house. He now files an application for a Certificate of Eviction against the tenant occupying the first floor apartment alleging that he wishes to occupy the entire house. The landlord claims that he need not establish the existence of an immediate and compelling necessity but only that he seeks possession in good faith of the first floor apartment. Will a Certificate of Eviction be granted? 

-24 

369

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

No. Even though the accommodations sought are located in a two-family house, the landlord must nevertheless establish the existence of an immediate and compelling necessity as a condition of obtaining a Certificate of Eviction in this case, since he is already in possession of one of the housing units in the structure. It is only when the owner of a two-family house does not occupy either apartment and seeks to secure possession of one of the units in the structure that he is not required to establish the existence of an immediate and compelling necessity. 

§ 55(1)—Certificates of Eviction Where a Landlord Desires to Exchange Apartments in a Two-Family House. Administrator's Opinion No. 

83 [Revoked. See Administrator's Opinion No. 118 Under Section 

54]. 

§ 55(1) Certificate of Eviction Where a Landlord Does Not Take Possession of Vacant Apartment in a Two-Family House but Permits a Relative to Move in. Administrator's Opinion No. 84. 

A landlord purchases a two-family house which has a vacancy on the second floor. He permits his brother to take possession of the vacant apartment. He now files an application for a Certificate of Eviction against the tenant occupying the first floor apartment alleging that he seeks in good faith possession for self-occupancy. May he obtain a Certificate?

No. The landlord should have relieved his housing necessity by occupying the vacant apartment. When he fails to do so he cannot thereafter be deemed in good faith in seeking the eviction of the tenant. Such a practice is an evasive one which is designed to circumvent the legislative Act and Regulations since the landlord could not have obtained possession of an apartment for a relative who is not a member of his "immediate family," as defined in the Regulations.

However, if the landlord had permitted his son to take 

370

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

possession of the vacant apartment it would be proper to conduct an inquiry into whether or not the son had had an immediate and compelling necessity prior to taking possession of the vacant apartment. If it is established that the son did have this immediate and compelling necessity it would be proper to grant the application since the landlord could first have moved into the vacant apartment and then properly filed an application for possession by his son. It would not constitute an evasion of the Regulations for the landlord to obtain the Certificate for his own occupancy instead of for his son's occupancy because the tenant would in any event have been evicted. However, if the son had not had an immediate and compelling necessity, the landlord's application would be denied since he could not be deemed in good faith in seeking the eviction of the tenant. 

His obvious purpose in permitting the son to take possession of the vacant apartment was to circumvent the requirement of the Regulations that a tenant may be evicted for occupancy by a member of the landlord's immediate family only where such member can establish an immediate and compelling necessity for such occupancy. 

§ 55(3)—Application for Certificate of Eviction by Owner of Cooperative Apartment. Administrator's Opinion No. 79. 

Stock in a cooperative corporation was purchased by a landlord which gave him a proprietary lease to an apartment. The landlord now desires to secure possession of the apartment for his own personal use and occupancy. Must he establish an immediate and compelling necessity in order to obtain a certificate of eviction?

The landlord has the same rights under the Regulations as the owner of a one-family house. He is not required to establish the existence of an immediate and compelling necessity for the apartment, but he must show that he is 371

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

proceeding in good faith and that he has complied with all other applicable requirements of Section 55 of the Regulations. 

However, where the landlord desires to recover possession of the apartment for the use and occupancy of a member of his immediate family, as that term is defined by the Regulations, the landlord must prove an immediate and compelling necessity for occupancy by such member of his immediate family. 

§ 55(3)-Cooperative Apartments-Computation of 80 Percent Requirement. Administrator's Opinion No. 81 [Revoked]. 

§ 55(4)-Payment of 20 Percent of Purchase Price. 

Opinion No. 30. 

Administrator's

At the time of the purchase of a house, the landlord did not comply with the requirement that he pay 20 percent of the purchase price, or the assessed valuation of the premises, whichever was the greater. Since that time he has reduced the mortgage so that his payments now total at least 20 percent. Does this meet the payment require ment of Section 55(4)? 

Yes. The test is whether the landlord has complied with the 20 percent requirement prior to the date of filing the application for a certificate of eviction. 

§ 55(4)—Who Must Pay 20 Percent of Purchase Price. Administrator's Opinion No. 31. 

A two-family house was purchased by co-owners. Possession of one apartment was secured by one of the coowners. The other co-owner files an application. The facts are that the co-owner now filing an application did not pay any part of the 20 percent down-payment. May a certificate be granted where the co-owner meets all other requirements of Section 55? 

Yes.

So long as there has been compliance with the re- 372

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

quirement of a 20 percent payment, there is no necessity for either one of the co-owners to show that he has paid his proportionate share of the down-payment. 

Section 56. Tenant not using premises for own dwelling. § 56-Landlord Claims Occupant to Be a Squatter. Administrator's Opinion No. 57.

A landlord claims that, without his knowledge or consent, a tenant now no longer in possession, permitted a stranger to occupy his apartment. The landlord wishes to evict the present occupant on the ground that he is a squatter. Is the landlord required to give any notice under the Rent and Eviction Regulations or to obtain a certificate of eviction prior to instituting legal action to evict the present occupant?

Where the landlord takes the position that the present occupant is a squatter, he need not serve any notice under Section 53 of the Rent and Eviction Regulations, nor need he obtain a certificate of eviction. The question as to whether or not an occupant is a squatter is one to be determined by the courts. Should the court rule that technically the occupant is not a squatter because of having entered lawfully into possession, and therefore rule that the landlord must proceed pursuant to the Rent and Eviction Regulations, the landlord may file an application under Section 56. Should the Local Rent Administrator then determine that the occupant did enter into possession without the consent or knowledge or acquiescence of the landlord, or of his duly authorized agent, and that the landlord has proceeded promptly with his attempt to evict the occupant, the Local Rent Administrator may issue a certificate permitting the eviction of the occupant upon such terms and conditions as he deems proper. 373

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS Section 57. Alteration or remodeling. 

§ 57-Application for Subdivision of Occupied Apartment. Administrator's Opinion No. 55. 

A landlord has filed an application for permission to subdivide a housing accommodation containing six or more rooms, exclusive of bathrooms and kitchen, occupied by a tenant. What considerations govern the issuance of an order of subdivision or certificate of eviction in such a case?

Where authorization to alter or remodel tenant-occupied housing accommodations is sought for the purpose of subdividing such housing accommodations into a greater number of self-contained family units each case will be determined upon the individual facts after applying the following general rules. 

The Local Rent Administrator must first determine whether the housing accommodation is under-occupied within the intent and meaning of Section 57. That section provides that "An apartment may be deemed under-occupied when there is less than one occupant for each room, exclusive of bathrooms, kitchen and three additional rooms. 

Roomers or boarders who are not members of the tenant's family shall not be counted as occupants."

It is within the discretion of the Local Rent Administrator to determine whether the housing accommodation is under-occupied after consideration of all the facts in each particular case. For example, the Administrator may determine that a full-time maid or servant who sleeps in is an occupant and part of the family unit for the purposes of this section.

If it is determined that the housing accommodations are not under-occupied, then the application for authorization to subdivide will be denied.

If it is determined that it is under-occupied, then further inquiry will be made. The landlord must establish that he 3.74

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

has filed the necessary plans for the alteration and has secured official approval and permits from all proper authorities. Only if under-occupancy and the adequacy of plans and approvals are established does the amount of the freeze date rent become pertinent.

If the maximum rent for the housing accommodations was $200 or more per month as of March 1, 1943, or the date of first renting thereafter, the Local Rent Administrator will issue a certificate of eviction permitting the landlord to pursue his remedies at law toward securing possession of the premises for the purposes of the subdivision. In such a case, the landlord is under no obligation to relocate the tenant in other accommodations or to offer the tenant any portion of the premises either during or after the subdivision. Such a tenant who is permitted by the landlord to occupy any of the housing accommodations resulting from the subdivision is protected only by the agreement between the parties without any control by the Administrator since, upon completion of the subdivision and application by the landlord, such housing accommodations will be decontrolled by order of the Local Rent Administrator.

If the maximum rent for the housing accommodation was less than $200 per month as of March 1, 1943, or the date of first renting thereafter, the Local Rent Administrator will determine whether one of the resulting units will adequately meet the housing needs of the tenant. The landlord's and tenant's expression as to suitability will be given due weight but the final determination must be made by the Local Rent Administrator. This will be reached after a careful consideration of the reasonable needs of the tenant and the members of his immediate family. The same considerations with respect to servants will be used in determining suitability as were used in determining underoccupancy. The size of the original housing accommoda- 375

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

tion does not necessarily determine the question of suitability of the unit resulting from the subdivision that is offered to the tenant. The Administrator will establish the rent to be paid by the tenant for such unit pursuant to Section 36(a) of the Regulations. 

Where the tenant remains in occupancy during the period of actual alteration, lower standards of suitability may be permitted during the period of alteration. The Local Rent 

Administrator will establish the rent to be paid by the tenant during the period of alteration. 

Where none of the units resulting from the alteration will be adequate, but the landlord can show that he is willing and able to relocate the tenant in other suitable accommodations, the Local Rent Administrator will determine whether such proposed relocation is in suitable housing accommodations within the same city, village or town, not unreasonably distant from the premises being altered and available at a rent not greater than the rent now being paid by the tenant. The tenant may not unreasonably refuse to accept the offer of relocation or fulfillment of any substitute requirement made by the Administrator pur suant to Section 54(5) of the Regulations. 

If the Local Rent Administrator determines that the needs of the tenant can be adequately met by occupancy of one of the units resulting from the alteration at a rental to be fixed by the Local Rent Administrator or that the tenant can be relocated in other adequate housing accommodations, an order authorizing the subdivision will be granted. If neither of these conditions can be complied with, the application will be denied. 

Where the landlord establishes that the alteration or remodeling cannot practicably be done with the tenant remaining in possession of any portion of the housing accommodation and that he is willing to relocate the ten- 376

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

ant during the period of the alteration, the tenant may not unreasonably refuse to accept this offer of temporary relocation. The Local Rent Administrator may thereupon issue an order permitting subdivision upon such terms and conditions as he may deem appropriate, including the right of the tenant to possession of one of the resulting units after completion of the alteration at a rental to be fixed by the Local Rent Administrator. 

The order in all cases of subdivision will grant the landlord permission to recover possession of the housing accommodations without further application to the Administrator where the tenant fails or refuses to abide by the terms and conditions included in the order of subdivision. Whether the tenant has unreasonably refused to abide by such terms and conditions is a question to be determined by the courts.

Section 59. Withdrawal of occupied housing accommodations from rental market. 

$59 and Former Sections 57(1) and 58(a)—New Procedure for Evic tion Pursuant to Certificate Previously Issued Upon the Ground of 

Illegal Occupancy. Administrator's Opinion No. 58 [Revoked]. 

§ 59(2)-Application for Withdrawal from the Rental Market on the Ground of Violations Where Landlord Can Secure Abatement of Taxes from the City of New York. Administrator's Opinion No. 113 (For New York City only). [Revoked: The Appellate Division, First 

Dept. has held in two recent cases that "it was erroneous on the part of the administrator to assume that a landlord's failure to seek abatement barred the granting of an application under section 59." 

(New Year Realty Corp v Herman, 201 NYS2d 226; Matter of 

Rosen, NYLJ June 29, 1960, p 7, col 2)].

Section 62, Evasion. 

§ 62-Illegality of Beverage Commission Exacted by Landlord or Agent. 

Administrator's Opinion No. 106. 

The landlord of a multiple family house is also a real estate broker licensed in the State of New York.

A pro- spective tenant wishing to rent an apartment in the build- 3.77

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

ing is advised by the landlord, that in addition to the maximum rent for the apartment, he will also be compelled to pay a broker's commission. The tenant rents the apartment and pays the first month's rent plus the commission demanded. The tenant then files a complaint with the 

Local Rent Administrator claiming that the commission which he paid represents an overcharge and is a violation of the Regulations. What is the position of the Administrator with respect to this complaint?

The collection by the landlord of more than the maximum rent for the apartment, even though this additional payment is claimed to be a broker's commission, is a violation by the landlord of Section 10 of the Emergency Housing Rent Control Law and Section 62 of the Rent and Eviction 

Regulations, which prohibits such evasive practices. 

It is also a violation by the landlord where the alleged broker's commission is paid to the superintendent, managing agent of the building or other representatives of the landlord even though they may be licensed real estate brokers.

Section 62 provides: "Evasion. The maximum rents and other requirements provided in these Regulations shall not be evaded, either directly or indirectly, in connection with the renting or leasing or the transfer of a lease of housing accommodations by requiring the tenant to pay or obligate himself for membership or other fees, or by modification of the practices relating to payment of commissions or other charges, or by modification of the services furnished or required to be furnished with the housing accommodations, or otherwise." 378

ADVISORY BULLETINS OF STATE RENT ADMINISTRATOR ADVISORY BULLETIN No. 1 (Issued April 16, 1956) INSTRUCTIONS TO LANDLORDS FOR THE PREPARATION OF APPLICATIONS AND REPORTS General Instructions 

(1) Use the right form. In order to simplify the selection of the right form the Commission has keyed the form number to the section of the Regulations under which the application is made. An application for an increase of maximum rent pursuant to Section 33(3) of the Regulations should be made on Form 33(3). An application for a prior opinion pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Regulations should similarly be made on Form 33(1)PO. The “PO” is an added key that the form deals with prior opinions. In other situations descriptive symbols are used to indicate the use to which the form should be made. 

As a further aid in the selection of the proper form a description of its use is set forth in the title. Form 33(1) 

is entitled "Landlord's Application for Increase of Maxi mum Rent (Substantial Rehabilitation; Major Capital Improvements; or Other Improvements)". This indicates that it is to be used for applications pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Regulations where the requested increase is based upon either a substantial rehabilitation, major capital improvement or other improvement.

If you will be guided by the fact that the form number is keyed to the section of the Regulations involved and that the title of the form is descriptive of its use, you will have little difficulty in selecting the form that fits your needs. 

-25 

385

(2) Supply all required information. Applications are rejected if essential information is lacking. This not only delays you because you will be required to refile a proper application; but, in addition wastes our time. Use care in preparing your applications in order that we may be able to serve you better.

Particular attention should be given to the blank spaces on the form. Complete all required information. In all cases the address and description of the housing accommodations involved must be clearly stated. The identification or description of the housing accommodations must correspond with the identification used on the registration statement on file with the Local Rent Office. It is also imperative to complete the information concerning the name and mailing address of both the landlord and tenant. In addition each form contains instructions. Read these instructions carefully and supply the information required. 

Remember care used at the outset will reduce delays to a minimum-the result will be an expeditious determination of your application. (3) Notarize your application before filing. Before filing your application you are required to sign the original and all copies. Only the original application need be sworn to before a Notary Public or other person authorized to administer oaths. The Commission has assigned personnel to each Local Rent Office for your convenience who are authorized to administer oaths. No charge is made for this service. (4) File the correct number of copies. The original application and at least one extra copy, together with accompanying documents, if any, must be filed. In addition the landlord is required to file an additional copy of the application for each housing accommodation affected by the 

386

application. There are filing instructions on each form. 

Check these instructions carefully before filing. (5) File at the correct Local Rent Office. File the application either by delivery or mailing to the Local Rent Office servicing the premises involved in the application. You will avoid delay by filing in the proper office. Section 8 of the Regulations defines the areas under the jurisdiction of the indicated Local Rent Offices. In the Metropolitan 

New York City area the name, jurisdiction, address and telephone number of each Local Rent Office is listed below.
